Hadronic vacuum polarization to three loops in chiral perturbation theory
Laurent Lellouch, Alessandro Lupo, Mattias Sjö, Kálmán Szabo, and Pierre Vanhove

Abstract
Hadronic vacuum polarization at low virtualities limits the precision of experimental tests of the standard model via important physical observables. Here we compute that effect in two-flavor chiral perturbation theory to three loops. Among the master integrals that describe the amplitude, six are elliptic functions of the momentum. Of these five are new to this work, although all can be related to the three-loop sunset integral. The renormalizability of the amplitude hinges on relations between the master integrals that were not previously known and that are not consequences of the integration-by-parts reduction. Our result is intended to serve as a starting point for phenomenological calculations, as well as the computation of finite-volume corrections in lattice QCD.



1
Introduction

The propagation of photons in the vacuum is modified by quantum fluctuations of the quark and gluon fields.
This phenomenon is known as hadronic vacuum polarization (HVP).
At low virtualities, our current knowledge of HVP limits the precision of experimental tests of the standard model via important observables such as the muon anomalous magnetic moment
Muong-2:2025xyk
or the value of the electromagnetic coupling at the electroweak scale
FCC:2018byv
.
To align the standard-model prediction for the muon
g-2
with the current experimental uncertainty
Muong-2:2025xyk
, the precision of the HVP contribution must improve by a factor of 4 relative to the world average of
Aliberti:2025beg
or by a factor of 2 compared to the result in ref.
Boccaletti:2024guq
.
Furthermore, stringent tests of the standard model via precision electroweak observables at future facilities such as CERN’s FCC-ee require reducing uncertainties by a factor of 3 on the running of
\alpha
at low virtualities
FCC:2018byv
.
To various degrees, HVP also enters the computation of quantities such as the anomalous magnetic moments of the electron and the
\tau
, as well as the ground-state hyperfine splitting of muonium and muonic helium (see e.g. ref.
Keshavarzi:2019abf
).

At low energies, the challenge of computing HVP stems from the fact that quark and gluon interactions are highly non-linear and not amenable to the usual perturbative methods of quantum field theory (QFT): the strong interaction, described by quantum chromodynamics (QCD), is nonperturbative in that regime.
There are two main approaches to addressing this challenge: massively parallel numerical simulations in a discretized version of QCD known as lattice QCD, and a data-driven approach.
The latter combines the measurement of the
e^{+}e^{-}\to\text{hadrons}
cross section as a function of center-of-mass energy with fundamental features of relativistic QFT, such as unitarity and analyticity properties of two-point Green’s functions (see e.g. ref.
Aliberti:2025beg
for a review).

A third approach is Chiral Perturbation Theory (ChPT)
Gasser:1983yg
;
Gasser:1984gg
.

{\mathrm{ChPT}}
is the low-energy effective field theory of QCD and, as such, is a key tool for studying hadronic interactions at low energies.
While the predictive power of this approach is limited by the appearance of an increasing number of
a priori
unknown couplings at each new loop order (low-energy constants, LECs), it does constrain the functional form of strong-interaction observables at low energies.
In particular, it provides important information about the very low-energy QCD contributions to HVP that are dominated by two-pion contributions.
This information can then be combined with the predictions of the main approaches to improve their accuracy.
Here we present a calculation of those contributions to three loops in
\mathrm{SU}(2)
ChPT
Gasser:1983yg
.

One motivation for this calculation is to have a precise, model-independent determination of the finite-volume corrections that have to be applied to lattice results for quantities sensitive to long-distance HVP effects, such as the HVP contribution to the muon anomalous magnetic moment.
Other model-independent approaches have also been proposed.
1
1

1

These are the Hansen-Patella method
Hansen:2019rbh
;
Hansen:2020whp
and the Meyer-Lellouch-Lüscher approach
Luscher:1990ux
;
Lellouch:2000pv
;
Meyer:2011um
.

It is understood that finite-volume effects arise from long-distance, low-energy hadronic fluctuations, making
\mathrm{SU}(2)
ChPT the natural framework to describe them
Aubin:2020scy
.
Intuitively, the lightest propagating states will be the most sensitive to the finite spacetime boundaries, since they have the slowest rate of exponential decay with the spatial size
L
of the lattice.
In the case of the isospin-
1
channel, which is the largest part of the HVP contribution to the muon
g-2
, these are states of two pions.

Finite-volume effects on the HVP contribution to the muon
g-2
have been computed to one
Aubin:2015rzx
and two
Aubin:2019usy
;
Borsanyi:2020mff
loops.
Assuming a geometric progression of these effects, the three-loop contribution and its uncertainty can be estimated, with the latter taken to be the sum of the remainder of the series.
Using the results in ref.
Borsanyi:2020mff
, one obtains
(17.9\pm 1.0)\times 10^{-10}
for lattices of spatial (and temportal) extent
L=6.3\,\mathrm{fm}
(
T=2L/3
) that are typical of those used in state-of-the-art computations.
This result is fully compatible with the direct lattice determination of that correction
Borsanyi:2020mff
, but with an uncertainty that is
2.5
times smaller.
Moreover, three loops is the first order in ChPT at which this uncertainty is smaller—by a factor of
1.5
—than the one on the current world average of measurements of the muon
g-2

Muong-2:2025xyk
, guaranteeing that this uncertainty is not a limiting factor in testing the Standard Model via this muon property.
Of course, such a determination will require knowledge of a number of LECs.
However, as discussed later, at three loops only six combinations of LECs contribute (three each from the NLO
Gasser:1983yg
and NNLO
Bijnens:1999sh
Lagrangians), all of which can be determined from quantities such as the pion vector form factor at two loops.
2
2

2

In ref.
Aubin:2020scy
it is shown that a new counterterm involving muon fields is needed in the three-loop
{\mathrm{ChPT}}
computation of
a_{\mu}
. However, because it is a contact term, it will only contribute to the FVEs on
a_{\mu}
to four loops. Moreover, no such counterterm is needed in the computation of the HVP function itself.

In the meantime, a precise lattice-plus-data-driven determination of the HVP contribution to
a_{\mu}
was obtained in
Boccaletti:2024guq
.
In that approach, FVEs were reduced by a factor of roughly 2 and their uncertainty by approximately 3.
This means that two-loop
{\mathrm{ChPT}}
should be sufficient to reach the precision required by experiment.
Nevertheless, a three-loop determination would provide an important cross-check of the corresponding uncertainty estimate.
Moreover, a number of lattice collaborations are currently calculating the HVP contribution to
a_{\mu}
fully on the lattice
Djukanovic:2024cmq
;
RBC:2024fic
;
FermilabLatticeHPQCD:2024ppc
and for those the improvement brought by a three-loop calculation is relevant.
In addition, such a calculation will reduce the uncertainty on lattice calculations of the low-energy running of
\alpha
.

As a first step in computing those FVEs, we calculate here HVP in infinite volume to three loops in ChPT.
Beyond the determination of FVEs, the results of this calculation are interesting in and of themselves.
They not only allow the study of the slope, but also of the curvature and turnaround of the HVP function for spacelike momenta.
In the timelike region, its imaginary part not only describes the interference between the one-
Gasser:1983yg
and two-loop
Bijnens:1998fm
pion form-factor contributions to the
e^{+}e^{-}\to\pi^{+}\pi^{-}
cross section, but also the four-pion contribution, which is absent at lower orders.
Moreover, it is only the second three-loop ChPT calculation performed
and therefore pushes the envelope of such computations.
3
3

3

The only other one that we know of is the three-loop computation of the pion mass and decay constant by Bijnens & Hermansson-Truedsson
Bijnens:2017wba
.

The evaluation of the three-loop amplitude presented in this work
requires state-of-the-art integration techniques of Feynman integrals
and the use of new identities between master integrals, which are not derivable from the commonly used
integration-by-part algorithms,
and which were previously unknown. The one- and two-loop
contributions to HVP only involve logarithms and polylogarithms, while the three-loop amplitude leads to elliptic integrals.

The elliptic master integrals needed in this work correspond to two-point functions with a single mass and no massless internal propagators.
Using Tarasov dimension shifting relations
Tarasov:1996br
, the elliptic master integrals are reduced to integrals in two dimensions that are both ultraviolet and infrared finite.
Compared to previous three-loop computations in other theories, the fact that all internal lines are massive here leads to new elliptic master integrals that where
unknown in the literature.
All the needed elliptic master integrals can be obtained by differentiation or integration of the three-loop sunset integral in two dimensions.
This integral arises in several precision calculations of the standard model
Groote:1998wy
;
Groote:2005ay
,
quark and gluon self-energies
Broadhurst:1993mw
,
QED calculations
Lee:2019wwn
;
Lee:2020mvt
;
Forner:2024ojj

and in Higgs boson decay amplitudes
Wang:2024ilc
.
It has been the subject of many studies, including refs.
Bailey:2008ib
;
Bloch:2014qca
;
Primo:2017ipr
;
Broedel:2019kmn
;
Pogel:2022yat
.

Using the algorithms developed in ref.
Lairez:2022zkj
;
delaCruz:2024xit
, we give the differential equations satisfied by the new elliptic master integrals.
The resolution of these differential equations provides fast and high precision evaluations of the master integral in the complex energy plane, and a complete control of the theoretical uncertainties at this order in ChPT.
The renormalization of the ultraviolet divergences of the three-loop amplitude is obtained thanks to the derivation of new relations between the elliptic master integrals evaluated in two dimensions.
These relations are not a consequence of integration-by-part identities as they arise in fixed spacetime dimension.
We provide two ways of deriving them, one based on the familiar Schouten identities
Tancredi:2015pta
and another using the differential equations satisfied by the master integrals.

While we give an analytic derivation of these elliptic master integrals here, we defer details concerning their numerical implementation to forthcoming work
LLSV
.
Likewise, due to the complexity of the present calculation, we focus here on the computation of the three-loop HVP amplitude itself,
postponing phenomenological applications and the computation of finite-volume effects to later work.

We conclude this introduction with a summary of earlier ChPT HVP calculations.
It was calculated at one-loop order in Gasser & Leutwyler’s original ChPT papers
Gasser:1983yg
;
Gasser:1984gg
.
The two-loop calculation was performed by Golowich & Kambor
Golowich:1995kd
and then at greater generality by Bijnens et al.
Amoros:1999dp
;
Bijnens:2011xt
.
Few calculations have been carried out to three-loop order in ChPT. As mentioned above, to the best of our knowledge, there is only this work and the pion mass and decay constant calculated by Bijnens & Hermansson-Truedsson
Bijnens:2017wba
.
Reproduction of the results of refs.
Bijnens:2011xt
;
Bijnens:2017wba
, whose
FORM

Vermaseren:2000nd
;
Ruijl:2017dtg
implementations have been made available to us, served as helpful verification throughout the present calculation.

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows.

Section

2
describes the relevant theoretical background and lists the Feynman diagrams.

Section

3
is devoted to the treatment of the loop integrals, with lengthy expressions and further mathematical details in
appendices

A
,
B
,
C
,
D
, and
E
.
We present the results in
section

4
, with further results in
appendix

F
.
A description of how the calculations were implemented can be found in
appendix

G
, along with links to our codes.

2
Theoretical background

The basic quantity of interest is the vacuum polarization

\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)\coloneq\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,e^{iqx}{\big\langle 0\big|\,T\big\{j^{\mu}(x)j^{\nu}(0)\big\}\big|0\big\rangle}\,,

(1)

where
j_{\mu}(x)
is the electromagnetic current.
HVP consists of the hadronic contributions to
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)
, i.e., those where the right-hand side is computed from some model of hadrons, for which we use two-flavor ChPT as described in detail below.
From now on, we will assume that
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)
contains only this hadronic part.

As a rank-
2
tensor depending on a single four-vector
q^{\mu}
, Lorentz invariance dictates that
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)
can be projected onto just two orthogonal structures,

\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)=(q^{\mu}q^{\nu}-q^{2}\eta^{\mu\nu})\Pi_{T}(q^{2})+q^{\mu}q^{\nu}\Pi_{L}(q^{2})\,,

(2)

where (in
d
dimensions with
\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}=d
)

\Pi_{L}(q^{2})\coloneq\frac{1}{(q^{2})^{2}}q_{\mu}q_{\nu}\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)\,,\qquad\Pi_{T}(q^{2})\coloneq\frac{1}{1-d}\bigg[\frac{1}{q^{2}}\eta_{\mu\nu}\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)-\Pi_{L}(q^{2})\bigg]

(3)

are its longitudinal and transverse components, respectively.
By the Ward–Takahashi identity,
\Pi_{L}(q^{2})=0
.
4
4

4

Most individual Feynman diagrams contributing to
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)
possess a longitudinal component, and the vanishing of
\Pi_{L}(q^{2})
only becomes manifest after summing all diagrams and performing the master integral reduction (see
section

3
).
Therefore, the Ward–Takahashi identity is a powerful sanity check on our calculations, ruling out most possible errors such as missing diagrams, erroneous symmetry factors, malformed Feynman rules, or misconfigured master integral reduction.

2.1
The ChPT Lagrangian

We use two-flavor ChPT in the isospin limit, describing the low-energy dynamics of a
\mathrm{SU}(2)
triplet of pion fields of mass
M_{\pi}
coupled to an external, non-dynamical photon field
A_{\mu}
.
We need the Lagrangian to the first four orders in the chiral power counting,

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ChPT}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{LO}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{NLO}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{NNLO}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{N^{3}LO}}+\cdots.

(4)

The pions are contained in a matrix field
U
which we parametrize as

U=\frac{i\Phi}{F_{0}\sqrt{2}}+\sqrt{1-\frac{{\langle\Phi^{2}\rangle}}{4F_{0}^{2}}}\,,

(5)

where
F_{0}
is the bare pion decay constant
and
\Phi=\sum_{i}\phi^{i}\sigma^{i}
is the matrix of pion fields.
We normalize our Lie algebra as
{\langle\sigma^{i}\sigma^{j}\rangle}=\delta^{ij}
, where
{\langle\cdots\rangle}
denotes an
\mathrm{SU}(2)
trace.
The same physical results are obtained from any parametrization that is equivalent to
eq.

5
up to
\mathcal{O}(\Phi^{2})
, such as
U=\exp(i\Phi/F_{0}\sqrt{2})
.

The leading Lagrangian is

\mathcal{L}_{\text{LO}}=\frac{F_{0}^{2}}{4}{\big\langle D_{\mu}U(D^{\mu}U)^{\dagger}+\chi U^{\dagger}+U\chi^{\dagger}\big\rangle}\,,

(6)

featuring the covariant derivative
D_{\mu}U\coloneq\partial_{\mu}U-ir_{\mu}U+iU\ell_{\mu}
.
In this calculation, the chiral gauge fields are simply
\ell_{\mu}=r_{\mu}=A_{\mu}Q
where
Q=\frac{e}{3}\diag(+2,-1)
is the quark charge matrix.
Likewise,
\chi
, which contains the quark mass matrix, is simply the bare pion mass
M_{0}
.
However, we retain
\ell_{\mu},r_{\mu}
and
\chi
to preserve the manifestly chiral-invariant appearance of the Lagrangian.
We also keep
\ell_{\mu}
and
r_{\mu}
because the axial current, proportional to
r_{\mu}-\ell_{\mu}
, determines the pion decay constant which appears in our calculation (see
section

2.4
).

The next-to-leading Lagrangian is (following ref.
Gasser:1983yg
but using somewhat modernized notation)
5
5

5

There is a subtlety here due to the distinct form of the original 2-flavor Lagrangian compared to the later 3- or
N
-flavor ones
Gasser:1984gg
.
If done too naïvely, a reduction from
N
to 2 flavors via the Cayley–Hamilton theorem leads to errors in the part of our result where
\mathcal{L}_{4}
enters quadratically (diagrams 10–20 in
table

2
) due to not fully taking into account the equations of motion remarked in ref.
Bijnens:1999hw
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{NLO}}

\displaystyle=\frac{l_{1}}{4}{\big\langle D_{\mu}U(D^{\mu}U)^{\dagger}\big\rangle}^{2}+\frac{l_{2}}{4}{\big\langle D_{\mu}U(D_{\nu}U)^{\dagger}\big\rangle}{\big\langle D^{\mu}U(D^{\nu}U)^{\dagger}\big\rangle}+\frac{l_{3}}{16}{\big\langle\chi U^{\dagger}+U\chi^{\dagger}\big\rangle}^{2}

(7)

\displaystyle+\frac{l_{4}}{2}{\big\langle D_{\mu}\chi^{\dagger}D^{\mu}U\big\rangle}+l_{5}{\big\langle U^{\dagger}F_{R}^{\mu\nu}UF_{L\mu\nu}\big\rangle}+i\frac{l_{6}}{2}\big[{\langle F_{R}^{\mu\nu}U_{\mu}U^{\dagger}_{\nu}\rangle}+{\langle F_{L}^{\mu\nu}U^{\dagger}_{\mu}U_{\nu}\rangle}\big]

\displaystyle-2h_{2}{\big\langle F_{R}^{\mu\nu}F_{R\mu\nu}F_{L}^{\mu\nu}F_{L\mu\nu}\big\rangle}+\cdots\,,

where the ellipses denote terms which do not appear in our calculation.
The parameters
l_{i}
and
h_{i}
are the bare LECs and the field strengths are, for our purposes,
F_{L}^{\mu\nu}=F_{R}^{\mu\nu}=Q(\partial^{\mu}A^{\nu}-\partial^{\nu}A^{\mu})
.
6
6

6

The Lagrangian is derived under the assumption that
Q
(and therefore
F_{L,R}^{\mu\nu}
) is traceless, which is not the case here.
However,
{\langle Q^{2}\rangle}
and
{\langle Q\rangle}^{2}
appear here only as
{\langle Q^{2}\rangle}-{\langle Q\rangle}^{2}
, so
the number of degrees of freedom is the same as if
{\langle Q\rangle}=0
.
Therefore, no additional terms are needed in the Lagrangian.

Note that the
h_{2}
term is a
contact term
and does not contain pion fields.

The lengthy higher-order Lagrangians
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{NNLO}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{N^{3}LO}}
are given in refs.
Bijnens:1999hw
;
Bijnens:2018lez
.
We have implemented the full ChPT lagrangian in
FORM

Vermaseren:2000nd
;
Ruijl:2017dtg
, and the attached code (see
appendix

G
) includes efficient on-the-fly derivation of any Feynman rule from
\mathcal{L}_{\text{LO}}+\cdots+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{N^{3}LO}}
.

2.2
Renormalization

The Lagrangians beyond
\mathcal{L}_{\text{LO}}
provide counterterms.
At NLO, the renormalization of the LECs in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions at a scale
\mu
(conventionally,
\mu=770\penalty\ \mathrm{Me\kern-1.00006ptV}\approx M_{\rho}
) is

l_{i}=\pi_{16}(c\mu)^{d-4}\Big[l^{q}_{i}(\mu)+\tfrac{\gamma_{i}}{d-4}\Big]\,,\qquad h_{i}=\pi_{16}(c\mu)^{d-4}\Big[h^{q}_{i}(\mu)+\tfrac{\delta_{i}}{d-4}\Big]\,,

(8)

where the
l^{q}_{i}(\mu)
are the renormalized LECs
and
\pi_{16}\coloneq\tfrac{1}{16\pi^{2}}
.
7
7

7

Note that in ref.
Bijnens:1999sh
, renormalized quantities are given as functions of
d
, which would imply that they possess an
\epsilon
expansion whose subleading terms would appear in diagrams containing both counterterms and loops.
However, such subleading terms can always be absorbed into the leading part of higher-order LECs, and this is indeed done.
(We thank Johan Bijnens for clarifying this point.)

We follow ref.
Bijnens:2017wba
in using a convention that more uniformly distributes powers of
\pi_{16}
; the conversion to that of, e.g., refs.
Bijnens:1999sh
;
Bijnens:2014lea
is
l_{i}^{r}(\mu)=\pi_{16}l_{i}^{q}(\mu)
.
The coefficients
\gamma_{i}
are
Gasser:1983yg

\gamma_{1}=\tfrac{1}{3}\,,\quad\gamma_{2}=\tfrac{2}{3}\,,\quad\gamma_{3}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,,\quad\gamma_{5}=-\tfrac{1}{6}\,,\quad\gamma_{6}=-\tfrac{1}{3}\,,\quad\gamma_{7}=0\,,\qquad\delta_{2}=-\tfrac{1}{12}\,.

(9)

The scheme-dependent coefficient
c
is conventionally
8
8

8

This is the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
scheme but with
-1
added in the exponent, which is the standard scheme for ChPT.

(c\mu)^{d-4}=e^{-\epsilon(\gamma_{E}+\log\mu^{2}-\log 4\pi-1)}=C(\epsilon,\mu^{2})e^{\epsilon}\,,

(10)

where
d=4-2\epsilon
and
\gamma_{E}
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant.
For later convenience, we have defined
C(\epsilon,z)\coloneq e^{-\epsilon(\gamma_{E}+\log z-\log 4\pi)}
(see
section

3
).

Similarly, the LECs
c_{i}
of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{NNLO}}
are renormalized as

c_{i}=\frac{\pi_{16}^{2}(c\mu)^{2(d-4)}}{F_{0}^{2}}\Big[c_{i}^{q}(\mu)-\tfrac{\gamma_{i}^{(2)}}{(d-4)^{2}}-\tfrac{\gamma_{i}^{(1)}+\gamma_{i}^{(L)}(\mu)}{\pi_{16}(d-4)}\Big]\,,

(11)

with coefficients listed in ref.
Bijnens:1999sh
.
The
\gamma_{i}^{(2)}
and tje
\gamma_{i}^{(1)}/\pi_{16}
are rational numbers like the
\gamma_{i}
, while the
\gamma_{i}^{(L)}/\pi_{16}
are rational linear combinations of the
l_{j}^{q}
.
The factor of
1/F_{0}^{2}
ensures that the physical LECs are dimensionless.

There currently exists no N
3
LO counterpart to ref.
Bijnens:1999sh
.
The renormalization of the N
3
LO LECs would look analogous to
eq.

11
, including a
1/(d-4)^{3}
term, with all coefficients unknown.
However, the N
3
LO LECs only appear in very simple terms in our amplitude and need only absorb a limited kind of local divergences.
Therefore, the coefficient combinations that appear can simply be inferred from whatever is left after the more involved NLO
+
NNLO renormalization.
We state these expressions in
section

4.2
.

Unmarked vertices are LO, dots NLO, and squares NNLO.
The rows and columns are numbered so that individual diagrams can be easily referenced, such as “14” for the double bubble.
The mirror images of the non-symmetric diagrams (03 and 12) are not listed independently.
Note that all 2-loop diagrams factor into 1-loop integrals; there are no sunset diagrams.

:
All N
3
LO diagrams, presented similarly to
table

1
.

3
LO.
The rows and columns are numbered so that individual diagrams can be easily referenced.
The mirror images of the non-symmetric diagrams (03, 13, 32–41, etc.) are not listed independently.
Note that, unlike at NNLO, there are six 3-loop diagrams (91–101) which do not factor into 1-loop integrals.

2.3
Diagrams

The power-counting scheme of ChPT can be informally stated as follows: for an N
k
LO diagram,
k
is the order in ChPT’s expansion parameters (not the number of loops).
For
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q^{2})
, the diagrams at NLO and NNLO are given in
table

1
, and those at N
3
LO in
table

2
.
In particular, note how the lack of vertices with an odd number of pion lines—a consequence of G-parity—strongly limits the available topologies and completely removes the two-loop sunset.

As is usual with ChPT calculations, the algebraic expressions corresponding to these diagrams are very lengthy, so we used
FORM 4.2

Vermaseren:2000nd
;
Ruijl:2017dtg
throughout our calculations.
To handle the large number of diagrams in a less error-prone way, we used a newly written program that, given a simple description of the diagram topologies, generates the
FORM
code that would in earlier work be written by hand.
See
appendix

G
for further information.

2.4
Mass and decay constant

The Lagrangian contains the bare pion decay constant,
F_{0}
, and all masses coming from vertices and propagators are nominally the bare pion mass,
M_{0}
.
These are related to their renormalized counterparts
F_{\pi}
and
M_{\pi}
(which are scheme-independent physical observables) via

M_{0}^{2}\to M_{\pi}^{2}\bigg[1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\xi^{i}\delta M_{i}\bigg]\,,\qquad F_{0}\to F_{\pi}\bigg[1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\xi^{i}\delta F_{i}\bigg]\,,\\

(12)

where
\xi\coloneq\pi_{16}M_{\pi}^{2}/F_{\pi}^{2}
and the expressions for
\delta M_{i}
and
\delta F_{i}
are given below.
An N
k
LO amplitude is then obtained by summing, over
k^{\prime}\leq k
, all contributing N
{}^{k^{\prime}}
LO amplitudes in which mass and decay constant renormalization has been performed to N
{}^{k-k^{\prime}}
LO, via the expressions above.

Given the lack of LO diagrams in our case, we only need mass and decay constant renormalization up to NNLO.
This was first determined by Bürgi
Burgi:1996qi
, although we use the form given along with the N
3
LO case in ref.
Bijnens:2017wba
:

\delta M_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{i}b^{M}_{ij}L_{\pi}^{i}\,,\qquad\delta F_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{i}b^{F}_{ij}L_{\pi}^{i}\,,

(13)

where
L_{\pi}\coloneq\log(M_{\pi}^{2}/\mu^{2})
and

\displaystyle b^{M}_{10}=-2l_{3}^{q}\,,\quad b^{M}_{11}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,,\qquad b^{F}_{10}=-l_{4}^{q}\,,\quad b^{F}_{11}=1\,,

(14)

\displaystyle b^{M}_{20}=-\tfrac{163}{96}-64c_{18}^{q}-32c_{17}^{q}-96c_{11}^{q}-48c_{10}^{q}+16c_{9}^{q}+32c_{8}^{q}+16c_{7}^{q}+32c_{6}^{q}

\displaystyle-4l_{3}^{q}l_{4}^{q}+8(l_{3}^{q})^{2}-2l_{2}^{q}-l_{1}^{q}\,,

\displaystyle b^{M}_{21}=\tfrac{13}{3}-l_{4}^{q}+11l_{3}^{q}+8l_{2}^{q}+14l_{1}^{q}\,,\qquad b^{M}_{22}=-\tfrac{5}{8}\,,

\displaystyle b^{F}_{20}=\tfrac{13}{192}-8c_{9}^{q}-16c_{8}^{q}-8c_{7}^{q}-(l_{4}^{q})^{2}+2l_{3}^{q}l_{4}^{q}+l_{2}^{q}+\tfrac{1}{2}l_{1}^{q}\,,

\displaystyle b^{F}_{21}=-\tfrac{29}{12}+3l_{4}^{q}-4l_{2}^{q}-7l_{1}^{q}\,,\qquad b^{F}_{22}=-\tfrac{1}{4}\,.

3
The loop integrals

In this section, we summarize the methods used to reduce and evaluate the loop integrals appearing in
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)
,
unifying the notation relative to the various sources from which they are taken.

3.1
Loop integral manipulations

To write down a family of
{N_{L}}
-loop integrals with loop momenta
\{\ell_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{L}}
and
{N_{p}}
independent external momenta
\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{p}}
,
one first constructs a set of
{N_{k}}
momenta
\{k_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N_{k}}
such that all propagators appearing in the integrals have the form

\frac{1}{D_{j}}\coloneq\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}-m_{j}^{2}+i\epsilon}\,,\qquad j\in\{1,\ldots,{N_{k}}\}\,,

(15)

and such that any product
\ell_{i}\cdot\ell_{j}
or
\ell_{i}\cdot p_{j}
can be written as a linear combination of inverse propagators
D_{j}
and the masses; this condition fixes
{N_{k}}={N_{p}}{N_{L}}+{N_{L}}({N_{L}}+1)/2
.
9
9

9

To see that the number of distinct propagators never exceeds this
{N_{k}}
,
consider a connected graph with
N_{V}
vertices,
N_{E}
internal edges and
{N_{L}}
loops,
ignoring 2-point vertices since these do not affect the number of distinct propagators.
Thus, each vertex has at least
3
legs, each of which is either an external leg or half an edge, so that
{N_{p}}+2N_{E}\geq 3N_{V}
.
Combining this with Euler’s formula,
N_{V}-N_{E}+{N_{L}}=1
, yields
N_{E}\leq 3({N_{L}}+1)+{N_{p}}
, which implies
N_{E}<{N_{k}}
for all
{N_{L}}\geq 1,{N_{p}}\geq 0
.

In the present case, we only need to consider
m_{j}=M_{\pi}
.
All of the integrals in
d
dimensions (which is taken to be an integer minus
2\epsilon
) can be written as

\big[\pi_{16}C(\epsilon,M_{\pi}^{2})\big]^{N_{L}}I^{(d)}_{\vec{\nu}}\big(\{p_{i}\cdot p_{j}\};M_{\pi}^{2}\big)\coloneq\int\hskip-3.22916pt\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{1}}{\pi^{d/2}}\cdots\int\hskip-3.22916pt\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{N_{L}}}{\pi^{d/2}}\prod_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\frac{1}{D_{j}^{\nu_{j}}}\,,

(16)

where
\vec{\nu}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N_{k}}
is a vector of integers that distinguishes members of the integral family.
This accommodates integrals with nontrivial numerators as long as they consist only of scalar products, since these can be accounted for with negative
\nu_{j}
.
The Wick rotation to Euclidean space takes the form
I_{\vec{\nu}}\to i^{N_{L}}(-1)^{\sum_{j}\nu_{j}}I^{E}_{\vec{\nu}}
.

Note in
eq.

16
how we use the same normalization factor
C(\epsilon,M_{\pi}^{2})
as in
eq.

10
, albeit with
M_{\pi}^{2}
instead of
\mu^{2}
; this simplifies dimensional regularization and also ensures that
I^{(d)}_{\vec{\nu}}
has integer mass dimension.
After the conversion
C(\epsilon,\mu^{2})=C(\epsilon,M_{\pi}^{2})e^{\epsilon L}
, all diagrams at N
k
LO will, when expressed in terms of
I_{\vec{\nu}}
and renormalized LECs, contain
C(\epsilon,M_{\pi}^{2})^{k}
, which can be kept as-is throughout the calculations until the
\epsilon\to 0
limit can be safely taken with
C(0,M_{\pi}^{2})=1
.
The
\pi_{16}(4\pi)^{\epsilon}
contained in the normalization of
I_{\vec{\nu}}
compensates for how we normalize with
\pi^{d/2}
in
eq.

16
(which simplifies many formulae below) rather than
(2\pi)^{d}
(which is how the integrals appear in the diagrams).

There exist a large number of techniques for manipulating loop integrals, and in
appendix

A
we provide some details on those used in this work.
In summary, we first use integration-by-parts relations to express all integrals in terms of a small number of
master integrals
listed below.
All subsequent calculation efforts can then be concentrated on these.
Tarasov’s dimensional shift
Tarasov:1996br
is then used to relate master integrals in
d
dimensions to those in
d-2
, simplifying renormalization since lower-dimensional integrals are typically less divergent.
We also derive novel Schouten relations which, in the vicinity of integer dimension, provide additional relations between the master integrals.
Lastly, we derive differential equations satisfied by the integrals, allowing them to be computed without explicitly integrating any loop momenta.

There exist several excellent programs for performing the reduction to master integrals, but in this work we exclusively use
LiteRed 2

Lee:2012cn
;
Lee:2013mka
,
10
10

10

We have unsuccessfully tried to perform the reduction also in other programs, but have been hampered by crashes or extremely long running times.
Out of the box,
LiteRed 2
requires
\mathcal{O}(\text{1 hour})
to determine the general reduction rules for our propagators, which needs to be done only once, and can then reduce all integrals in
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)
in
\mathcal{O}(\text{1 minute})
.

along with
pySecDec

Borowka:2017idc
;
Borowka:2018goh
;
Heinrich:2021dbf
;
Heinrich:2023til
for numerically verifying the various integral manipulations.

3.2
Master integrals of
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q^{2})
at three-loop order

We use the following basis, which allows writing all three-loop integrals appearing in
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)
:

\{k_{j}\}_{j=1}^{9}=\left\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\quad\ell_{1}-q,\ell_{2}-q,\ell_{3}-q,\quad\ell_{1}+\ell_{3},\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\right\}\,.

(17)

For 2-loop integrals, we use the subset
\{k_{1},k_{2},k_{4},k_{5},k_{9}\}
,
and for 1-loop integrals,
\{k_{1},k_{4}\}
.
In order to simplify our expressions, we write the master integrals as functions of the dimensionless parameter
t\coloneq q^{2}/M_{\pi}^{2}
, suppress their dependence on
M_{\pi}
, and render all master integrals dimensionless at
d=2
with an appropriate power of
M_{\pi}
.

At one-loop order, the set of master integrals is simply the tadpole (
) and bubble (

\begin{gathered}I^{(d)}_{1,1,0,0,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})=T_{20}(d)\,,\qquad M_{\pi}^{2}I^{(d)}_{1,1,1,0,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})=T_{11}(d;t)\,,\\

At three loops, we have 11 master integrals.
Of these, four are again factorizable into tadpole-bubble products:

\displaystyle I^{(d)}_{1,1,1,0,0,0,0,0,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle=T_{30}(d)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{4}I^{(d)}_{1,1,1,1,1,0,0,0,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle=T_{12}(d;t)\,,

(24)

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{2}I^{(d)}_{1,1,1,1,0,0,0,0,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle=T_{21}(d;t)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{6}I^{(d)}_{1,1,1,1,1,1,0,0,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle=T_{03}(d;t)\,.

The remaining ones are highly nontrivial, and we name them as follows:
12
12

12

For comparison, ref.
Bijnens:2017wba
has two such non-factorizable three-loop master integrals (plus one at two loops, namely the sunset
I_{1,0,0,1,1}
).
In our notation, they are
E_{0}
and
I_{1,1,0,0,0,1,1,1,0}
,
the latter of which does not appear in our set.
Both are known from earlier gauge theory calculations
Melnikov:2000zc
.

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{2}I^{(d)}_{1,1,0,0,0,0,1,1,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle\eqcolon E_{0}(d)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{4}I^{(d)}_{1,1,0,1,0,0,1,1,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle\eqcolon E_{4}(d;t)\,,

(25)

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{2}I^{(d)}_{1,0,0,0,1,0,1,1,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle\eqcolon E_{1}(d;t)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{6}I^{(d)}_{1,1,0,1,1,0,1,1,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle\eqcolon E_{5}(d;t)\,,

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{4}I^{(d)}_{2,0,0,0,1,0,1,1,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle\eqcolon E_{2}(d;t)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{8}I^{(d)}_{2,1,0,1,1,0,1,1,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle\eqcolon E_{6}(d;t)\,,

\displaystyle M_{\pi}^{6}I^{(d)}_{3,0,0,0,1,0,1,1,0}(q^{2},M_{\pi})

\displaystyle\eqcolon E_{3}(d;t)\,,

where “
E
” refers to the fact that their evaluation involves elliptic integrals (see
appendix

E
).
The vacuum integral
E_{0}(d)
is simply
E_{1}(d;0)
, and
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
can be related to the first and second
t
-derivatives of
E_{1}
(see
appendix

B
); likewise,
E_{6}
is related to the derivative of
E_{5}
.
The general layout of the integrals is illustrated in
fig.

1
.

Figure 1
:
From left to right: the basic topology and momentum routing common to all
E_{i}
integrals, corresponding in that form to the vacuum integral
E_{0}
; the same with an external momentum
q
routed through the thicker line, giving

Each line is tagged with its index

: it carries momentum

given in
eq.

17
and occurs with power
\nu_{i}
in
I_{\vec{\nu}}
.

All
E_{i}
are divergent in four dimensions but finite in two, so we employ Tarasov’s dimensional shift to extract their divergences.
Going forward, we use the following notation for the master integrals as series in
\epsilon
:

E_{i}(4-2\epsilon;t)=\sum_{r=-3}^{\infty}\epsilon^{r}E_{i}^{(r)}(4;t)\,,\qquad E_{i}(2-2\epsilon;t)=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\epsilon^{r}E_{i}^{(r)}(2;t)\,.

(26)

Since the integrals are finite in two dimensions, we simply have
E_{i}(2;t)=E_{i}^{(0)}(2;t)
, whereas we refer to the finite part in four dimensions as
\bar{E}_{i}(4;t)\coloneq E_{i}^{(0)}(4;t)
.

4
Results

In this section, we present our main result, namely
\Pi_{T}(q^{2})
[i.e., the transverse component of
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q^{2})
as defined in
eqs.

1
and
3
], computed to N
3
LO as outlined in previous sections.
Besides being a function of the pion mass
M_{\pi}^{2}
, the pion decay constant
F_{\pi}^{2}
and the photon momentum
q^{\mu}
through
t\coloneq q^{2}/M_{\pi}^{2}
, it also contains the renormalized low-energy constants
l_{i}^{q}
and
c_{i}^{q}
, as well as
r_{i}^{q}
, which are defined below in
section

4.2
.
These are all functions of the renormalization scale
\mu
in the customary ChPT renormalization scheme, while
\Pi_{T}
itself is kept scale-independent through the appearance of
L_{\pi}\coloneq\log\frac{M_{\pi}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}
, which in turn emerges from the mass and decay constant renormalization, and from reconciling the factors of
C(\epsilon,\mu^{2})
in the LECs with those of
C(\epsilon,M_{\pi}^{2})
in the loop integrals (see
section

3.1
).
The constant
\zeta(3)=1.202\ldots
also appears.

The
t
-dependence of the result is expressed using the functions

{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.9 0 L 6.26 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

defined in
eq.

and evaluated in
section

;
E_{1}(2;t)
,
E_{2}(2;t)
and
E_{3}(2;t)
defined in
eq.

25
, shifted to
2
dimensions using the method described in
section

A.2
and evaluated in
appendix

E
;
\bar{E}_{5}(4;t)
and
\bar{E}_{6}(4;t)
defined in
eq.

26
and evaluated in
appendix

D
.
The reason for using a mix of
2
- and
4
-dimensional integrals was explained in
section

A.3
.

We have subjected the result to the following checks:

•

The longitudinal part
\Pi_{L}(q^{2})
vanishes, as required by the Ward–Takahashi identity;

•

All non-local divergences are removed by the renormalization, and the local ones are of a form that allows them to be canceled by the N
3
LO counterterms (see
section

4.2
);

•

The result is invariant under reparametrization of the Nambu–Goldstone manifold, e.g., replacing
eq.

5
by
U=e^{i\Phi/F_{0}\sqrt{2}}
, or more generally following ref.
(
Bijnens:2022zsq
, app. B)
;

•

There are no singularities at
t=0
despite the presence of factors of
\frac{1}{t}
;
13
13

13

That such factors appear can be viewed as a consequence of our choice of master integrals (and
J_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to4.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-2.45348pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

).
While a choice that absorbs them would certainly be possible—compare the use of
\overline{B}_{21}
rather than the scalar bubble integral in ref.

—we have found it simpler to retain the “natural” master integrals, at the cost of having to take the
t\to 0
limit more carefully.

•

The above checks hold also when the calculation is repeated at
3
or
N
mass-degenerate flavors, to ensure that no errors have been made whose effects just happen to vanish in the
2
-flavor case;

•

Restricting the calculation to NLO or NNLO reproduces earlier results
Gasser:1983yg
;
Gasser:1984gg
;
Golowich:1995kd
;
Amoros:1999dp
;
Bijnens:2011xt
; see
eqs.

28
and
29
.

4.1
Expressions for the HVP

As discussed above, the longitudinal component,
\Pi_{L}(q^{2})
, vanishes identically, so we only consider the transverse component
\Pi_{T}(q^{2})
, which we renormalize on shell, as done in most physical applications. Thus, we present results for
\bar{\Pi}_{T}(q^{2})\coloneq\Pi_{T}(q^{2})-\Pi_{T}(0)
,
deferring discussion of the unsubtracted quantity
\Pi_{T}(q^{2})
to
appendix

F
.
We expand the result in powers of
\xi
,

\frac{1}{\pi_{16}}\bar{\Pi}_{T}(q^{2})=\bar{\Pi}_{T}^{\mathrm{NLO}}(t)+\xi\bar{\Pi}_{T}^{\mathrm{NNLO}}(t)+\xi^{2}\bar{\Pi}_{T}^{\mathrm{N^{3}LO}}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{3})\,,

(27)

where, as in
section

2.4
,
\xi\coloneq\pi_{16}M_{\pi}^{2}/F_{\pi}^{2}\approx 0.03
and
\pi_{16}\coloneq\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}
.
Note that on the right-hand side, we deliberately write the
\bar{\Pi}
’s as dimensionless functions of
t
.

The first two terms in
eq.

27
are known from earlier work
Gasser:1983yg
;
Gasser:1984gg
;
Golowich:1995kd
;
Amoros:1999dp
;
Bijnens:2011xt
, but we restate them here in our notation:
14
14

14

Refs.
Golowich:1995kd
;
Amoros:1999dp
;
Bijnens:2011xt
express the non-polynomial
t
-dependence in terms of the functions
\overline{B}_{21}
and
\overline{B}_{22}
, which derive from the finite part of the rank-
2
tensor bubble integral.
Here, “finite part” is defined by writing the divergence in terms of
\lambda_{0}=\big[1+\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}\big]C(\epsilon,M_{\pi}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
.

4.2
N
3
LO renormalization

Unlike at lower order
Gasser:1983yg
;
Bijnens:1999sh
, the explicit renormalization of the N
3
LO LECs has not been worked out in the literature.
However, these LECs only enter through the contact diagram 00 in
table

2
, which evaluates to a quadratic polynomial in
t
:

(36)

With
\tilde{c}_{n}
the LEC of term
n
in the 2-flavor N
3
LO Lagrangian
Bijnens:2018lez
, the coefficients
r_{i}
are

\begin{gathered}-r_{0}=64\big(\tilde{c}_{382}+\tilde{c}_{383}\big)+4\tilde{c}_{457}+8\big(\tilde{c}_{458}+\tilde{c}_{474}\big)+16\tilde{c}_{475}\,,\\
r_{1}=16\big(\tilde{c}_{332}-\tilde{c}_{333}\big)\,,\qquad r_{2}=-8\tilde{c}_{459}\,,\end{gathered}

(37)

which we symbolically renormalize via [compare
eqs.

8
and
11
]

r_{i}=\pi_{16}^{3}(c\mu)^{3(d-4)}\bigg[r_{i}^{q}(\mu)+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{\gamma_{ij}}{(d-4)^{j}}\bigg]\,.

(38)

The leading divergence of the sum of diagrams 01–101 in
table

2
is
\frac{8t^{2}}{81\epsilon^{3}}
, so we must have
\gamma_{03}=\gamma_{13}=0
and
\gamma_{23}=-\tfrac{8}{81}
in order to cancel this divergence.
16
16

16

Since
r_{2}
only contains one LEC, the contact term
\tilde{c}_{459}\big({\langle D^{2}F_{L}^{\mu\nu}D^{2}F_{L\mu\nu}\rangle}+{\langle D^{2}F_{R}^{\mu\nu}D^{2}F_{R\mu\nu}\rangle}\big)
is being the first (and, at the time of writing, only) term in the N
3
LO Lagrangian
Bijnens:2018lez
to be explicitly renormalized.

Likewise, from the subleading divergences we deduce

\displaystyle\gamma_{01}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{208}{27}-\tfrac{16}{3}l_{2}^{q}-\tfrac{8}{3}l_{1}^{q}-32l_{3}^{q}l_{6}^{q}+64l_{3}^{q}l_{5}^{q}-64c_{50}^{q}+256c_{47}^{q}-128c_{46}^{q}+64c_{44}^{q}

\displaystyle-64c_{35}^{q}+224c_{34}^{q}+16c_{33}^{q}-24c_{32}^{q}-24c_{31}^{q}+32c_{30}^{q}-96c_{29}^{q}-384c_{6}^{q}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{02}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{184}{9}-16l_{6}^{q}+32l_{5}^{q}-16l_{2}^{q}+32l_{1}^{q}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{11}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1013}{972}+8(l_{6}^{q})^{2}+\tfrac{256}{3}c_{53}^{q}-64c_{51}^{q}-64c_{50}^{q}+\tfrac{32}{3}c_{35}^{q}+\tfrac{128}{3}c_{6}^{q}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{12}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{128}{81}+\tfrac{16}{3}l_{2}^{q}-\tfrac{32}{3}l_{1}^{q}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{21}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{169}{5832}-\tfrac{4}{3}(l_{6}^{q})^{2}-\tfrac{32}{3}c_{53}^{q}+\tfrac{32}{3}c_{51}^{q}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{22}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{8}{243}+\tfrac{8}{9}l_{6}^{q}-\tfrac{4}{9}l_{2}^{q}+\tfrac{8}{9}l_{1}^{q}\,.

This makes our result finite.

Dependence on the LECs

The values of the NLO LECs appearing in our result are
Bijnens:2014lea

As can be seen in

\Pi_{T}(q^{2})
.
Note that refs.

;

, etc.)
use the “

l_{i}^{r}=\pi_{16}l_{i}^{q}
(recall
section

).

l_{1}^{q}=-0.65\pm 0.10\,,\quad l_{2}^{q}=0.273\pm 0.033\,,\quad l_{4}^{q}=0.92\pm 0.20\,,\quad l_{6}^{q}=-1.96\pm 0.07\,.

However, as can be seen in

,

and

(l_{2}^{q}-2l_{1}^{q}-2l_{6}^{q})=5.9\pm 0.4\,,\qquad(2l_{1}^{q}-l_{2}^{q}-2l_{6}^{q})=2.8\pm 0.4\,.

(40)

While all NLO LECs are known to some precision, the NNLO LECs are generally not.
Fortunately,

[recall

35

, and also

can be determined from measurements of the pion charge radius
NA7:1986vav

, although it is dominated by uncertainties in the NLO contributions and therefore not very accurate:
18

18

Again, note the normalization convention:
r_{Vi}^{r}=\pi_{16}^{2}r_{Vi}^{q}
.
Since the publication of the LEC values used here, there has been significant progress in the measurement of the observables from which they are determined (see, e.g., ref.
Leplumey:2025kvv
for the pion charge radius).
Thus, it should be possible to improve the precision of
r_{Vi}^{q}
.

r_{V1}^{q}=-5\pm 8\,,\qquad\qquad r_{V2}^{q}=4.0\pm 1.2\,.

(41)

Compare the estimate
r_{V1}^{q}\approx-6.2
from vector meson dominance

.

and therefore do not contribute to the imaginary part, and since they combine with the entirely undetermined N

.
However, in the computation of FVEs only terms associated with loops survive.
In the last line of
eq.

35
, these are
-32tL_{\pi}c_{50}^{q}
(from diagram 01 in
table

2
) and
16tL_{\pi}c_{56}^{q}
(via a tadpole loop in the decay constant renormalization), so for FVEs only the combination
2c_{50}^{q}-c_{56}^{q}
is needed.
These LECs appear in different linear combinations at two loops in the vector-vector and axial-axial correlators (with
c_{50},c_{56}
appearing as
C_{87},C_{93}
at three flavors
Amoros:1999dp
and as
K_{109},K_{115}
at
n
flavors
Bijnens:2011xt
), so their values can be determined if needed.

5
Conclusions and outlook

We have computed the three-loop HVP amplitude
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)
in two-flavor ChPT.
We have given the result in terms of three (poly)logarithmic and five elliptic functions of the photon virtuality, with four of the elliptic functions being new to this work.
While the polynomial part of the amplitude depends on entirely unknown LECs, the (poly)logarithmic part depends on only five parameters, all more or less known from data, and the elliptic part has no free parameters.
Our result provides the next-to-next-to-leading contribution to the subthreshold part of the amplitude and its two-pion cut, and the leading contribution to the four-pion cut, which comes entirely from the elliptic part.

The process of evaluating the amplitude has required unexpectedly deep forays into the field of loop integrals.
Satisfying the Ward–Takahashi identity required state-of-the-art master integral reduction, and renormalization required a step beyond that with the novel Schouten relations.
While relatively accessible methods allowed all our elliptic integrals to be related to a single one,
E_{1}
, which is well-studied and amenable to powerful mathematical approaches, the sheer complexity of putting this to use has prompted us to split off the numerical implementation into a follow-up work
LLSV
in order to keep the present scope manageable.
As it stands, the amplitude can be evaluated using
pySecDec

Borowka:2017idc
;
Borowka:2018goh
;
Heinrich:2021dbf
;
Heinrich:2023til
, although the precision is very limited for many virtualities, particularly above threshold.

The HVP amplitude, in particular when completed in ref.
LLSV
, serves as a starting point for the wide range of phenomenological applications surveyed in the introduction.
Not least among these are FVEs, and even if the most direct approach then is to recompute the amplitude in a finite volume, this completed infinite-volume calculation can serve as a blueprint for that undertaking.
This work also leaves us in a better position to consider if there are other three-loop ChPT calculations that may prove both useful and tractable.

Appendix A
More on loop integral manipulations

We apply various techniques to reduce the number of loop integrals and to compute them, some rather standard and others less so.
While the explicit expressions resulting from these techniques are lengthy enough that we defer them to their own appendices, this appendix is intended to provide an overview.
We use the notation of
section

3
throughout, and abbreviate
eq.

16
as
I_{\vec{\nu}}=\int J_{\vec{\nu}}
.

The common theme of these techniques is the application of differential operators to the integrands (see, e.g., refs.
Smirnov:2012gma
;
Travaglini:2022uwo
for expository texts).
Perhaps the most familiar example is integration by parts (IBP), namely the identity

0=\int\frac{\partial}{\partial\ell_{i}^{\mu}}\big(r^{\mu}J_{\vec{\nu}}\big)\,,

(42)

where
\ell_{i}
is any of the loop momenta and
r
is any momentum, loop or external.
Evaluating the derivative gives a linear combination of integrands
J_{{\vec{\nu}}^{\prime}}
for various
{\vec{\nu}}^{\prime}
, so each choice of
({\vec{\nu}},\ell_{i},r)
gives a linear combination of integrals
I_{{\vec{\nu}}^{\prime}}
that is known to vanish.
The resulting system of equations can be solved systematically using the Laporta algorithm
Laporta:2000dsw
, of which programs such as
LiteRed

Lee:2012cn
;
Lee:2013mka
are essentially implementations with various heuristic improvements.
Thus, any Feynman integral
I_{\vec{\nu}}
,
{\vec{\nu}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{{N_{k}}}
can be expressed in terms of a
finite
basis of master integrals
Smirnov:2010hn
;
Lee:2013hzt
;
Bitoun:2017nre
.

A.1
Differential equations

A useful property of derivatives with respect to masses and momenta is that they can only change the power to which an existing propagator appears in the integrand, but never make a new propagator appear.
This produces a hierarchy among the master integrals based on the propagators they contain, which in our three-loop case looks schematically like

}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\,.

(43)

Here,

A
B

means that the set of propagators in
B
is a subset of those of
A
, and we say that
B
is lower in the hierarchy.
Applying a differential operator to a master integral gives, after reduction, a linear combination of master integrals at the same level or lower in the hierarchy as the original integral, never higher.

A salient case is
\partial/\partial t
, or in general the derivative with respect to any kinematical invariant.
Say that
\{\mathscr{M}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are the master integrals living on some level in the hierarchy.
Then, schematically,

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathscr{M}_{i}(d;t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\rho_{ij}(d;t)\mathscr{M}_{j}(d;t)+\mathscr{S}_{i}(d;t)\,,

(44)

where
\rho_{ij}
are rational functions and
\mathscr{S}_{i}
contains only integrals lower in the hierarchy.
Taking further derivatives produces the same general structure as
eq.

44
, yielding a system of relations that allow the other
\mathscr{M}_{j}
,
j\neq i
to be traded for derivatives of
\mathscr{M}_{i}
.
Solving the system to eliminate the other
\mathscr{M}_{j}
gives the inhomogeneous differential equation

\sum_{j=0}^{n}\pi_{ij}(d;t)\bigg[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg]^{j}\mathscr{M}_{i}(d;t)=\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{i}(d;t)\,,

(45)

where
\pi_{ij}(d;t)
are polynomials, and the inhomogeneous part
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{i}
consists of integrals lower in the hierarchy.

Solving such a differential equation (with the simpler integral obtained at
t=0
as initial condition) is a viable alternative to direct integration [see, e.g., ref.
Bloch:2014qca
;
Broedel:2019kmn
;
Pogel:2022yat
, which yield
E_{1}(2;t)
].
We list these equations (with
n=3
for
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
,
n=2
for
E_{5},E_{6}
, and
n=1
for
E_{4}
) in
appendix

B
.
The method outlined here is a simple way of obtaining differential equations satisfied by the master integrals in general dimensions
Primo:2017ipr
. In fixed dimensions one needs to resort to the Picard-Fuchs algorithm
Lairez:2022zkj
;
delaCruz:2024xit
.
This algorithm also gives a direct way of deriving the differential equations satisfied by a given Feynman integral without requiring an explicit master integral reduction.

A.2
Tarasov’s dimensional shift

A more straightforward differential operator is
\partial/\partial m^{2}_{i}
, if we treat all propagators as having distinct masses; the equal-mass limit can be taken after it has been applied.
It acts as

\frac{\partial}{\partial m^{2}_{i}}\frac{1}{D_{j}^{\nu}}=\frac{\nu\delta_{ij}}{D_{j}^{\nu+1}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial m^{2}_{i}}I_{\vec{\nu}}=\nu_{i}I_{{\vec{\nu}}+\hat{\imath}},

(46)

where the
i
th component of
\hat{\imath}
is
1
and the others
0
.

Following a trick due to Tarasov
Tarasov:1996br
, this operator is utilized to formally change the dimension of an integral in steps of
2
.
The formula is best introduced by first bringing the integrals to parametric form
by using the identity

\Gamma(\nu)\coloneq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\alpha^{\nu-1}e^{-\alpha}=D^{\nu}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\alpha^{\nu-1}e^{-\alpha D}\,,

(47)

where in the second equality we shifted
\alpha\to\alpha D
.
19
19

19

Note that this assumes
D>0
, which is only guaranteed in Euclidean space.
However, since Wick rotation corresponds to a simple multiplicative factor for the integrals, the same identities hold also for the Minkowski-space integrals.

This gives

I_{\vec{\nu}}^{(d)}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to51.1529pt{\hfil$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{233013}{\hbox{$\textstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.65979pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hfil$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}$\hfil}\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{163108}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 21.92891pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{116506}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.07648pt}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\vec{\nu}}\int\hskip-3.22916pt\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{1}}{\pi^{d/2}}\cdots\int\hskip-3.22916pt\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{N_{L}}}{\pi^{d/2}}\exp\bigg[-\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\alpha_{j}D_{j}\bigg]\,,

(48)

where for brevity, we have defined the “Schwinger integral”

\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to51.1529pt{\hfil$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{233013}{\hbox{$\textstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.65979pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hfil$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}$\hfil}\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{163108}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 21.92891pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{116506}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.07648pt}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\vec{\nu}}\coloneq\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{1}^{\nu_{1}-1}}{\Gamma(\nu_{1})}\cdots\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\alpha_{N_{k}}\,\alpha_{N_{k}}^{\nu_{N_{k}}-1}}{\Gamma(\nu_{N_{k}})}\,.

(49)

Let us write [recall
eq.

15
]

\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\alpha_{j}D_{j}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N_{L}}A_{ij}[\ell_{i}\cdot\ell_{j}]+2\sum_{i=1}^{N_{L}}B_{i}\cdot\ell_{i}+C\,,

(50)

where
B
and
C
depend on the momenta
p_{j}
, and
C
also on the masses in the form
-\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}m_{j}^{2}
, whereas
A
is a constant matrix.
By shifting
\ell_{i=1}^{N_{L}}\to\ell_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{N_{L}}A_{ij}B_{j}
to complete the square in the exponential, one obtains a Gaussian integral that can be easily performed, leaving

I_{\vec{\nu}}^{(d)}=\frac{1}{\pi^{{N_{L}}d/2}}\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to51.1529pt{\hfil$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{233013}{\hbox{$\textstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.65979pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hfil$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}$\hfil}\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{163108}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 21.92891pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{116506}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.07648pt}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\vec{\nu}}\big[\det(\pi A^{-1})\big]^{d/2}\exp\bigg[\sum_{i,j=1}^{N_{L}}A^{-1}_{ij}B_{i}B_{j}-C\bigg]=\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to51.1529pt{\hfil$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{233013}{\hbox{$\textstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.65979pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hfil$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}$\hfil}\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{163108}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 21.92891pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{116506}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.07648pt}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\vec{\nu}}\frac{e^{-\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}+i\epsilon}}{\mathcal{U}^{d/2}}\,,

(51)

where
\mathcal{U}\coloneq\det(A)
and
\mathcal{F}\coloneq\mathcal{U}\big(\sum_{ij}A^{-1}_{ij}B_{i}B_{j}-C\big)
are the
Symanzik polynomials
; they are polynomials in
\{\alpha_{j}\}
and are homogeneous of degree
{N_{L}}
and
{N_{L}}+1
, respectively.
20
20

20

We use the sign convention of
LiteRed

Lee:2012cn
;
Lee:2013mka
for the second Symanzik polynomial, which differs by a sign from the definition used in ref.
Bloch:2014qca
.

Now, note that
m_{i}^{2}
only appears as the term
\alpha_{i}m_{i}^{2}
inside the exponent
\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}
, so the effect of
\partial/\partial m_{i}^{2}
on the right-hand side of
eq.

51
is simply to bring down a factor of
\alpha_{i}
inside the integrand.
Next, note that
d
only appears as the exponent in
\mathcal{U}^{d/2}
, and recall that
\mathcal{U}
is just a sum of products of
\alpha_{i}
’s; explicitly, let us write it as
\mathcal{U}\eqcolon U(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{N_{k}})
.
Then, maintaining that same functional form, it follows that

\mathcal{D}\coloneq U\Big(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial m_{1}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial m_{2}},\ldots,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial m_{N_{k}}}\Big)

(52)

is a differential operator such that

\mathcal{D}I_{\vec{\nu}}^{(d)}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to51.1529pt{\hfil$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{233013}{\hbox{$\textstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.65979pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hfil$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}$\hfil}\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{163108}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 21.92891pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{116506}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.07648pt}}\mkern-3.0mu\int\frac{U(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{N_{k}})e^{-\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}+i\epsilon}}{\mathcal{U}^{d/2}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to51.1529pt{\hfil$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{233013}{\hbox{$\textstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.65979pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hfil$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}$\hfil}\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{163108}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 21.92891pt}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to48.97934pt{\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{116506}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}}}$\hfil}\hss}\kern 22.07648pt}}\mkern-3.0mu\int\frac{e^{-\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}+i\epsilon}}{\mathcal{U}^{d/2-1}}=I_{\vec{\nu}}^{(d-2)}\,.

(53)

On the other hand,
\mathcal{D}I_{\vec{\nu}}^{(d)}
is a linear combination of the
d
-dimensional master integrals.
This produces a linear system that can be inverted to give the
d
-dimensional master integrals in terms of the less divergent
(d-2)
-dimensional ones.
We present the relations for our case in
appendix

C
.

A particularly elegant way of deriving
\mathcal{D}
is the well-known (see ref.
Nakanishi:1971
) graph-based determination of
\mathcal{U}
.
Consider the graph
G
representing a given integral (as in
fig.

1
) and consider all ways of removing
{N_{L}}
edges from the graph in such a way that the remaining edges form a connected tree graph that joins all vertices, which is called a
spanning tree
.
Then

\mathcal{U}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spanning}\\
\text{trees }T\end{subarray}}\quad\prod_{j\in G\setminus T}\alpha_{j}\,,

(54)

where
G\setminus T
is the set of
{N_{L}}
edges that were removed to form the spanning tree
T
.
As above,
\mathcal{D}
is obtained by replacing
\alpha_{j}\to\partial/\partial m_{j}^{2}
in this formula.

Appendix

C
details the application of this method to our master integrals.

A.3
Schouten relations

An important thing to note is that the set of master integrals is only minimal and irreducible with respect to IBP relations.
When working at fixed dimension or with fixed kinematics, additional relations may emerge that are not taken into account during master integral reduction.
A simple example is how
I_{\hbox to5.12pt{\vbox to5.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

E_{1}

t=0
, even though it is formally an independent master integral.
However, the present amplitude involves less obvious relations, as can be inferred from renormalizability.
Taking the sum of diagrams 91–101 in
table

2
at face value, its divergent part contains the integrals
E_{n}(2;t)
,
21
21

21

The reason for this can be seen in
appendix

, specifically
eqs.

92
and
93
, where the coefficients relating
E_{5,6}(4-2\epsilon;t)
to

diverge, introducing the finite integrals
E_{n}(2;t)
also into the divergent part.
This is not the case for
E_{0}
through
E_{4}
.

but since these are elliptic functions of

, no combination of rational functions and polylogarithms (which is what diagrams 00–90 provide) can cancel them.
Thus, there must be some additional relation between the elliptic master integrals that makes this problematic divergence vanish.

Such subtle and novel relations indeed hold for
E_{5}
and
E_{6}
, and can be viewed as consequences of Schouten
22
22

22

Pronounced [sxA

U

ı

t@n].
We make this point because difficult and oft-mangled names is a matter close to the heart of the author whose surname is pronounced [˚ø:].

identities; by analogy, we refer to these master integral relations as
Schouten relations
.
Schouten identities hold only at fixed integer dimension, and while the relations can be extended to the
\epsilon
expansion around that dimension and translated to other dimensions through dimension shifting, they are not detected by the IBP relations, which operate in general
d
.
23
23

23

We thank Roman Lee and Lorenzo Tancredi for discussions regarding this point.

The role of Schouten identities in reducing the number of independent master integrals was first noticed by Remiddi & Tancredi
Remiddi:2013joa
in the context of two-loop sunset integrals with three distinct masses, and is discussed further in ref.
Tancredi:2015pta
.
We have checked all relations numerically with
pySecDec

Borowka:2017idc
;
Borowka:2018goh
;
Heinrich:2021dbf
;
Heinrich:2023til
.

In the remainder of this section, we will discuss Schouten relations from three angles, ranging from the more profoundly connected to its underlying cause, to the more practically applicable to the case at hand.

A Schouten identity is the statement that the Gram determinant of
n
vectors vanishes in
d<n
dimensions, since no more than
d
vectors may be linearly independent.
In two dimensions, for three arbitrary vectors
u
,
v
and
w
, this takes the form

\mathbb{G}(u,v,w)\coloneq\begin{pmatrix}u^{2}&u\cdot v&u\cdot w\\
v\cdot u&v^{2}&v\cdot w\\
w\cdot u&w\cdot v&w^{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\quad\Rightarrow\quad|\mathbb{G}(u,v,w)|=0\qquad\text{at $d=2$.}

(55)

With
u,v,w
drawn from the loop and external momenta,
|\mathbb{G}(u,v,w)|
is a cubic polynomial in the
D_{j}
of
eq.

15
which vanishes at
d=2
.
Inserting that as the numerator of a suitably chosen integral and reducing the result yields a
(d=2)
-specific linear relation among the master integrals—a Schouten relation.

However, the above argument is only valid if the integral of interest is finite—the requirement that
d=2
precludes dimensional regularization—and in most cases where a finite integral is obtained, the resulting linear combination is zero in any dimension, yielding no new information.
However, a finite yet nontrivial result in our basis of propagators is obtained from
I^{(d)}_{1,1,0,1,1,0,2,1,0}
and
|\mathbb{G}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})|
: the integral

G(d;t)\coloneq\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{1}\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{2}\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{3}}{\pi^{3d/2}}\frac{|\mathbb{G}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})|}{D_{1}D_{2}\,D_{4}D_{5}\,D_{7}^{2}D_{8}}

(56)

is finite at
d=2
and reduces (using
LiteRed

Lee:2012cn
;
Lee:2013mka
) to

-12t(t+4)E_{4}(2;t)+96(t-1)tE_{5}(2;t)-48t^{2}(t-4)E_{6}(2;t)\\

due to the presence of

Using this removes all elliptic functions from the

A more systematic way of finding Schouten identities at any order in
\epsilon
comes from the differential equation for
E_{5}
.
In two dimensions, the second-order differential operator factorizes into two first-order ones; the explicit expressions are given in
eq.

72
, but the schematic form is

\Delta_{L}\Delta_{R}E_{5}(2;t)=\mathscr{S}_{5}\,,

(58)

where
\Delta_{L,R}
are linear differential operators and
\mathscr{S}_{5}
consists of integrals lower in the hierarchy of
eq.

43
.
In fact, the same holds perturbatively in
\epsilon
:

\Delta_{L}\Delta_{R}E_{5}^{(r)}(2;t)=\mathscr{S}_{5}^{(r)}\,,

(59)

where
r
denotes the order in
\epsilon
as given in
eq.

26
and
\mathscr{S}_{5}^{(r)}
consists of integrals lower in the hierarchy and of
E_{5}^{(r^{\prime})}(2;t)
and
E_{6}^{(r^{\prime})}(2;t)
with
r^{\prime}<r
.

Now, given
\Sigma^{(r)}
such that
\Delta_{L}\Sigma^{(r)}=\mathscr{S}_{5}^{(r)}
, we clearly get

\Delta_{R}E_{5}^{(r)}(2;t)=\Sigma^{(r)}\,,

(60)

which is a linear differential equation; in fact, the
r=0
version is the very same equation as that obtained through Griffiths-Dwork reduction
Lairez:2022zkj
;
delaCruz:2024xit
.
It can be contrasted with the plain derivative [explicitly,
eq.

75
]

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}E_{5}^{(r)}(2;t)=\rho(t)E_{6}^{(r)}(2;t)+\tilde{\Sigma}^{(r)}\,,

(61)

where
\rho
is a rational function and
\tilde{\Sigma}^{(r)}
, like
\Sigma^{(r)}
, is lower in the hierarchy or lower in the
\epsilon
expansion.
Canceling
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}E_{5}^{(r)}(2;t)
between

60
and
61
gives a linear relation that for

is equivalent to
eq.

57
.

(62)

and eliminates the
1/\epsilon
pole in the same way that
eq.

57
eliminated the
1/\epsilon^{2}
one.
The procedure can in principle be carried on to all orders in
\epsilon
, but the challenge is the highly nontrivial step from
\mathscr{S}^{(r)}
to
\Sigma^{(r)}
.
Nonetheless, this approach is interesting enough to warrant further study, since it can be applied to any master integral whose differential equation factorizes.

Ultimately, the most straightforward way of obtaining Schouten relations is to compare different ways of performing the
\epsilon
expansion.
This obscures the origin of the relations and only works for the divergent part, but is sufficient for our purposes.
Thus, we examine the expansions

\displaystyle E_{5}(4-2\epsilon;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{E_{5}^{(-3)}(4;t)}{\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{E_{5}^{(-2)}(4;t)}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{E_{5}^{(-1)}(4;t)}{\epsilon^{1}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\,,

(63)

\displaystyle E_{6}(4-2\epsilon;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{E_{6}^{(-2)}(4;t)}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{E_{6}^{(-1)}(4;t)}{\epsilon^{1}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\,,

where the values of
E_{5,6}^{(-r)}(4;t)
can be found by setting
d=4-2\epsilon
in
eqs.

92
and
93
; this way, all but the
1/\epsilon^{3}
divergences contain elliptic master integrals.
However, they can also be found by plugging
eq.

63
into the differential equations for
E_{5}
in
eq.

72
and
E_{6}
in
eq.

75
, where much of the complexity diverts to the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
terms.
Inserting the
\epsilon
expansion for the lower-hierarchy integrals
T_{21}
,
T_{12}
,
E_{0}
,
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
near four dimensions given in
sections

D.2
,
D.3
,
D.4
, and
D.5
, we deduce that the coefficients of the poles of
E_{5}
and
E_{6}
can be written entirely in terms of

.

The Schouten relations make it natural to express the amplitude in terms of
\bar{E}_{5}(4;t)
and
\bar{E}_{6}(4;t)
, since in two dimensions they eliminate
E_{6}(2;t)
and
E_{6}^{(1)}(2;t)
but leave more problematic functions like
E_{6}^{(2)}(2;t)
.
Using the four-dimensional integrals becomes even more natural in the entirely four-dimensional approach of
eq.

63
.
On the other hand, the integrals that are not the subject of Schouten relations—
E_{0}
through
E_{4}
—are more naturally used in their finite two-dimensional form.
This mixed two- and four-dimensional representation has the further benefit that
E_{4}
completely drops out of the expressions, as shown in
section

C.3
.

(66)

B.2
Differential equations for
E_{1},E_{2}
and
E_{3}

The master
E_{1}(d;t)
satisfies the third-order differential equation [compare
eq.

45
]

\bigg[\pi_{13}(d;t)\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial t^{3}}+\pi_{12}(d;t)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}+\pi_{11}(d;t)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\pi_{10}(d;t)\bigg]E_{1}(d;t)=-12(d-2)^{3}T_{30}(d)\,,

(67)

with polynomial coefficients

\displaystyle\pi_{13}(d;t)

\displaystyle=4(t-16)(t-4)t^{2}\,,

(68)

\displaystyle\pi_{12}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-12t\big[(d-4)t^{2}-10(d-5)t-64\big]\,,

\displaystyle\pi_{11}(d;t)

\displaystyle=(d-4)(11d-36)t^{2}-4(7d^{2}-88d+216)t-64(d-4)d\,,

\displaystyle\pi_{10}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-(d-3)(3d-8)\big[(d-4)t+2d+4\big]\,.

The masters
E_{2}(d;t)
and
E_{3}(d;t)
are obtained from the derivatives of
E_{1}(d;t)
using

\displaystyle E_{2}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\bigg[-\frac{t}{4}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\frac{3d-8}{8}\bigg]E_{1}(d;t)\,,

(69)

\displaystyle E_{3}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\bigg[\frac{t}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}+\frac{4d+(4-d)t}{16}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\frac{(d-4)(3d-8)}{32}\bigg]E_{1}(d;t)\,.

(70)

Below, we often use this to eliminate
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
in favor of derivatives of
E_{1}
.

B.3
Differential equation for
E_{4}

The master
E_{4}(d;t)
satisfies the first-order differential equation

\bigg[2(4-t)t\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+(4+(d-4)t)\bigg]E_{4}(d;t)=(d-2)E_{0}(d)+\bigg[\frac{d-4}{2}-3t\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg]E_{1}(d;t)\,.

(71)

In fact, we do not use this equation here but include it for completeness.

B.4
Differential equations for
E_{5}
and
E_{6}

The master
E_{5}(d;t)
satisfies the second-order differential equation

\bigg[\pi_{52}(d;t)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}+\pi_{51}(d;t)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\pi_{50}(d;t)\bigg]E_{5}(d;t)=\bigg[\tilde{\pi}_{12}(d;t)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}+\tilde{\pi}_{11}(d;t)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\tilde{\pi}_{10}(d;t)\bigg]E_{1}(d;t)\\
-24(d-3)(3d-8)E_{4}(d;t)+6(3d-8)(d-2)E_{0}(d)\\
-6(d-2)^{2}(t-2)T_{21}(d;t)+12(d-3)(d-2)tT_{12}(d;t),

(72)

with polynomial coefficients

\displaystyle\pi_{52}

\displaystyle=12(t-4)^{2}t^{2}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}_{12}

\displaystyle=2t(t+2)(t+8)\,,

(73)

\displaystyle\pi_{51}

\displaystyle=-24(t-4)t\big[(d-5)t+11-2d\big]\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}_{11}

\displaystyle=-(t+8)\big[(5d-16)t-2d\big]\,,

\displaystyle\pi_{50}

\displaystyle=3(t-4)\big[3(d-6)(d-4)t+8(2d-7)\big]\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}_{10}

\displaystyle=(3d-8)\big[(d-3)t-2(2d-3)\big]\,.

It should be noted that the differential operator on the left-hand side of
eq.

72
factorizes in two dimensions, with
\epsilon
corrections that are at most linear differential operators:

\bigg[\pi_{52}(2-2\epsilon;t)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}+\pi_{51}(2-2\epsilon;t)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\pi_{50}(2-2\epsilon;t)\bigg]=12(t-4)\bigg[t\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+1\bigg]\circ\bigg[t(t-4)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-3(t-2)\bigg]\\
+\bigg[48(t-4)(2t-4)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+4(15t-8)\bigg]\epsilon+12t\epsilon^{2}\,.

(74)

The implications of this are discussed in
section

A.3
.

The master
E_{6}(d;t)
is obtained from the derivative of
E_{5}(d;t)
using

E_{6}(d;t)=-\bigg[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\frac{1}{t}\bigg]E_{5}(d;t)+\bigg[\frac{t-16}{48(d-3)}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}-\frac{(5d-16)t+16d}{96(d-3)t}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\frac{3d-8}{96t}\bigg]E_{1}(d;t)\\
+\frac{3d-8}{8t}E_{4}(d;t)-\frac{(d-2)^{2}}{16(d-3)t}T_{21}(d;t)\,.

(75)

B.5
Initial conditions

When working with the above differential equations, it is useful to know the values of the master integrals at
t=0
.
Assuming the integrals to be sufficiently regular, this can be determined by setting
q=0
, which following
eq.

17
replaces
\{D_{4},D_{5},D_{6}\}\to\{D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\}
, and then applying the master integral reduction.
Since we have only two
t
-independent master integrals, the result must be of the form

E_{i}(d;0)=\mu_{i}(d)E_{0}(d)+\tilde{\mu}_{i}(d)T_{30}(d)\,,

(76)

where
24
24

24

Despite the appearance of
1/(d-4)
multiplying the already cubically divergent integrals
E_{0}
and
T_{30}
, the quartic (and for
E_{3}
and
E_{6}
, also cubic) divergences cancel, as can be seen by combining
eq.

76
with the expressions in
appendix

D
.

\begin{gathered}\mu_{1}(d)=1\,,\qquad\mu_{2}(d)=\mu_{4}(d)=\frac{3d-8}{8}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mu}_{1}(d)=\tilde{\mu}_{2}(d)=\tilde{\mu}_{4}(d)=0\,,\\
\begin{aligned} \mu_{3}(d)&=\frac{(d-7)(3d-10)(3d-8)}{128(d-4)}\,,&\qquad\tilde{\mu}_{3}(d)&=\frac{3(d-2)^{3}}{64(d-4)}\,,\\
\mu_{5}(d)&=\frac{(d-3)(3d-10)(3d-8)}{64(d-4)}\,,&\qquad\tilde{\mu}_{5}(d)&=-\frac{(d-2)^{3}}{32(d-4)}\,,\end{aligned}\\
\begin{aligned} \mu_{6}(d)&=\frac{3(d-6)(d-3)(3d-10)(3d-8)}{1024(d-4)}\,,\\
\tilde{\mu}_{6}(d)&=\frac{(d-2)^{3}(5d-14)}{512(d-4)}\,.\end{aligned}\end{gathered}

(77)

Along with derivative relations such as

eqs.

69
,
70
, and
75
, this gives a complete set of
initial conditions for the differential equations.

Expanding these relations near four dimensions and using the expansion
of
E_{0}(4-2\epsilon)
given in
eq.

103
and
T_{30}(4-2\epsilon)
given
in
eq.

102
, we have

\displaystyle E_{1}(4-2\epsilon;0)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{23}{3\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{\pi^{2}+35}{2\epsilon}+\frac{275+23\pi^{2}-24\zeta(3)}{12}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

(78a)

\displaystyle E_{2}(4-2\epsilon;0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{7}{3\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{12+\pi^{2}}{4\epsilon}+\frac{7\pi^{2}}{12}-\zeta(3)-\frac{5}{3}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

(78b)

\displaystyle E_{3}(4-2\epsilon;0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{5}{6\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{5}{2\epsilon}-\frac{35}{6}-\frac{5\pi^{2}}{24}+\frac{7\zeta(3)}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

(78c)

\displaystyle E_{4}(4-2\epsilon;0)

\displaystyle=E_{2}(4-2\epsilon;0)\,,

(78d)

\displaystyle E_{5}(4-2\epsilon;0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{4+\pi^{2}}{12\epsilon}+\frac{1}{3}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}-\frac{8\zeta(3)}{3}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

(78e)

\displaystyle E_{6}(4-2\epsilon;0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{4\epsilon}-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{16}+\frac{7\zeta(3)}{8}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,.

(78f)

We may also perform the expansion around
t=0
of the finite master integrals to higher order by finding power-series solutions to the differential equations:

\displaystyle E_{1}(2;t)

\displaystyle=-7\zeta(3)+\frac{6-7\zeta(3)}{16}t+\frac{54-49\zeta(3)}{1024}t^{2}+\mathcal{O}(t^{3}),

(79a)

\displaystyle E_{2}(2;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{7\zeta(3)}{4}+\frac{7\zeta(3)-6}{32}t+\frac{147\zeta(3)-162}{4096}t^{2}+\mathcal{O}(t^{3}),

(79b)

\displaystyle E_{3}(2;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{6-35\zeta(3)}{32}+\frac{102-105\zeta(3)}{512}t+\frac{458-399\zeta(3)}{8192}t^{2}+\mathcal{O}(t^{3}),

(79c)

\displaystyle\bar{E}_{5}(4;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}-\frac{8\zeta(3)}{3}+\bigg[\frac{17}{96}-\frac{35}{64}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\bigg]t+\mathcal{O}(t^{2}),

(79d)

\displaystyle\bar{E}_{6}(4;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{16}+\frac{7\zeta(3)}{8}+\Big[-\frac{\pi^{2}}{96}+\frac{1295\zeta(3)}{768}-\frac{23993}{31104}\Big]t+\mathcal{O}(t^{2})\,.

(79e)

Appendix C
Dimension-shifting relations

In this appendix we provide explicit expressions for the dimension-shifting relations derived using the methods described in
section

A.2
.
Since the tadpole and bubble integrals do not simplify significantly in two dimensions, we keep them four-dimensional and only apply the dimension shift to the
E_{i}
.
We will express the relations using the notation

E_{i}(d;t)=\sum_{a,b}(d-2)^{a}\tau_{i}^{ab}(d;t)T_{ab}(d;t)+\sum_{j}\varepsilon_{i}^{j}(d;t)E_{j}(d-2;t)\,,

(80)

with the coefficients
\tau_{i}^{ab}(d;t)
and
\varepsilon_{i}^{j}(d;t)
given below, again grouped by the hierarchy
eq.

43
.
Note that
eq.

53
gives
E_{j}(d-2;t)
in terms of
E_{i}(d;t)
, so
eq.

80
requires the inverse of that relation.
We keep
d
general here; the
\epsilon
expansion around
d=4
can be found in
appendix

D
.

C.1
Dimension shifting for the master
E_{0}

This is a simple case that is good for familiarizing oneself with the method described in
section

A.2
.

(81)

Reading the line indices off
fig.

1
,
eq.

54
gives

\mathcal{U}=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{7}\alpha_{8}\Big(\tfrac{1}{\alpha_{1}}+\tfrac{1}{\alpha_{2}}+\tfrac{1}{\alpha_{7}}+\tfrac{1}{\alpha_{8}}\Big)\,,

(82)

and the operator
\mathcal{D}
follows from
\alpha_{j}\to\partial/\partial m_{j}^{2}
.
Running this through
eq.

53
gives
eq.

80
with coefficients

\kappa_{0}(d)\tau_{0}^{30}(d;t)=\frac{2(11d-38)}{3}\,,\qquad\kappa_{0}(d)\varepsilon_{0}^{0}(d;t)=\frac{128(d-4)}{3}\,,

(83)

where we have pulled out a common denominator
\kappa_{0}(d)\coloneq(d-3)(3d-10)(3d-8)
.
Here and below, we omit coefficients
\tau_{i}^{ab}
and
\varepsilon_{i}^{j}
that vanish identically.
Note that since
\varepsilon^{0}_{0}(d;t)
vanishes at
d=4
,
E_{0}(2)
does not appear in
E_{0}(4-2\epsilon)
until the first order in
\epsilon
.

C.2
Dimension shifting for the masters
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
and
E_{3}

Here,
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{D}
are the same as for
E_{0}
, except that index
2
is replaced by
5
; compare
fig.

1
.
The resulting coefficients in
eq.

80
for
E_{1}
are

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}(d)\tau_{1}^{30}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{48}t^{2}+\frac{11d^{2}-85d+164}{6}t+\frac{2(38d^{2}-268d+473)}{3}\,,

(84a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}(d)\varepsilon_{1}^{1}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{5d^{2}-47d+110}{24}t^{3}+\frac{2(15d^{2}-128d+275)}{3}t^{2}-\frac{2(276d^{2}-2342d+5027)}{3}t

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{32(20d^{2}-178d+385)}{3}+\frac{512(3d^{2}-26d+56)}{3}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(84b)

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}(d)\varepsilon_{1}^{2}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d-6}{24}t^{4}+\frac{d-10}{6}t^{3}-\frac{2(97d-487)}{3}t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{16(73d-354)}{3}t+\frac{128(29d-119)}{3}-\frac{4096(d-4)}{3}\frac{1}{t-4}\,,

(84c)

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}(d)\varepsilon_{1}^{3}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}t^{4}-\frac{10}{3}t^{3}+112t^{2}-\frac{640}{3}t-\frac{2048}{3}\,,

(84d)

with
\kappa_{1}(d)\coloneq(d-3)(2d-7)(2d-5)(3d-10)(3d-8)
.

Those for
E_{2}
are

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}(d)\tau_{2}^{30}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{9d^{2}-70d+136}{48}t+\frac{54d^{2}-383d+680}{12}\,,

(85a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}(d)\varepsilon_{2}^{1}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(33d^{2}-295d+662)}{24}t^{2}+\frac{(-108d^{2}+921d-1996)}{6}

\displaystyle\quad-8(9d^{2}-80d+175)+\frac{128(3d^{2}-26d+56)}{3}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(85b)

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}(d)\varepsilon_{2}^{2}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{5(d-6)}{24}t^{3}+\frac{254-49d}{6}t^{2}+\frac{2(33d-179)}{3}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{32(31d-131)}{3}-\frac{1024(d-4)}{3}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(85c)

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}(d)\varepsilon_{2}^{3}(d;t)

\displaystyle=t^{3}+14t^{2}-\frac{512}{3}\,,

(85d)

with
\kappa_{2}(d)\coloneq(d-3)(2d-7)(3d-10)(3d-8)
, and those for
E_{3}
are

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}(d)\tau_{3}^{30}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{32}t+\frac{69d^{3}-753d^{2}+2732d-3296}{48(2d-7)}\,,

(86a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}(d)\varepsilon_{3}^{1}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{6d^{2}-55d+126}{24}t^{2}+\frac{-111d^{3}+1454d^{2}-6270d+8896}{24(2d-7)}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{-264d^{3}+3077d^{2}-11907d+15288}{6(2d-7)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{8(21d^{3}-254d^{2}+1016d-1344)}{3(2d-7)}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(86b)

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}(d)\varepsilon_{3}^{2}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d-6}{24}t^{3}+\frac{-73d^{2}+646d-1368}{24(2d-7)}t^{2}+\frac{21d^{2}-264d+676}{6(2d-7)}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{10(47d^{2}-355d+664)}{3(2d-7)}-\frac{64(7d^{2}-52d+96)}{3(2d-7)}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(86c)

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}(d)\varepsilon_{3}^{3}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}t^{3}+\frac{33d-116}{6(2d-7)}t^{2}+\frac{2(3d-8)}{3(2d-7)}t-\frac{32(7d-24)}{3(2d-7)}\,,

(86d)

with
\kappa_{3}(d)\coloneq(d-3)(3d-10)(3d-8)
.
