(87)

giving

\mathcal{U}=\alpha_{2}\alpha_{7}\alpha_{8}\Big[1+(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{4})\Big(\tfrac{1}{\alpha_{2}}+\tfrac{1}{\alpha_{7}}+\tfrac{1}{\alpha_{8}}\Big)\Big]\,.

(88)

The coefficients are

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}(d)\tau_{4}^{30}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{48}t-\frac{16d^{2}-115d+207}{6}\,,

(89a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}(d)\tau_{4}^{21}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-3)(2d-7)(11d-38)}{2}\,,

(89b)

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}(d)\varepsilon_{4}^{0}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{32(d-4)(2d-7)}{3}\,,

(89c)

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}(d)\varepsilon_{4}^{1}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{5d^{2}-47d+110}{24}t^{2}+\frac{-24d^{2}+263d-678}{6}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{8(37d^{2}-320d+693)}{3}-\frac{128(3d^{2}-26d+56)}{3}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(89d)

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}(d)\varepsilon_{4}^{2}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d-6}{24}t^{3}+\frac{74-13d}{6}t^{2}+\frac{2(61d-315)}{3}t

\displaystyle\quad-32(9d-41)+\frac{1024(d-4)}{3}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(89e)

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}(d)\varepsilon_{4}^{3}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}t^{3}+6t^{2}-64t+\frac{512}{3}\,,

(89f)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{4}(d)\varepsilon_{4}^{4}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{32(d-4)}{3}t+\frac{128(d-4)}{3}\,,

(89g)

with
\kappa_{4}(d)\coloneq(d-3)^{2}(2d-7)(3d-10)(3d-8)
and
\kappa^{\prime}_{4}(d)\coloneq(d-3)^{2}(9d^{2}-54d+80)
.

An interesting thing to note here is that
\varepsilon_{4}^{4}(4;t)=0
, so
E_{4}(2;t)
is not needed in order to evaluate
\bar{E}_{4}(4;t)
.
This does not necessarily mean that
E_{4}
is eliminated by the dimension shift, though, since it still appears in
E_{5}
and
E_{6}
(see below) as well as in
E_{i}^{(r)}(4;t)
for
r>0
.
However, this is why it does not appear in
eq.

31
.

and

(90)

plus horizontal and vertical reflections thereof.
Thus,

\mathcal{U}=\alpha_{7}\alpha_{8}\big[\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}\big]+(\alpha_{7}+\alpha_{8})\big[\alpha_{1}\alpha_{5}+\alpha_{2}\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{2}\alpha_{5}\big]\,.

(91)

The coefficients are

\displaystyle\kappa_{5}(d;t)\tau_{5}^{30}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(d-4)^{3}}{96(d-5)(d-3)^{2}}t^{4}+\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{24(d-3)^{2}}t^{3}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{9d^{5}-191d^{4}+1613d^{3}-6755d^{2}+14002d-11480}{12(d-5)(d-3)^{2}(2d-7)(3d-10)}t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{-72d^{4}+1075d^{3}-5981d^{2}+14700d-13468}{3(d-3)^{2}(6d^{2}-41d+70)}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{4(d-4)^{2}(39d^{2}-261d+434)}{3(d-3)^{2}(6d^{2}-41d+70)}\,,

(92a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{5}(d;t)\tau_{5}^{21}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-4)^{3}}{48(d-5)(d-3)}t^{4}-\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{12(d-3)}t^{3}+\frac{-7d^{3}+100d^{2}-475d+748}{6(d^{2}-8d+15)}t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{4(15d^{2}-125d+258)}{3(d-3)}t-\frac{8(5d^{2}-41d+84)}{d-3}\,,

(92b)

\displaystyle\kappa_{5}(d;t)\tau_{5}^{12}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{8(d^{2}-9d+20)}{3}t^{2}-\frac{4(23d^{2}-195d+410)}{3}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{16(11d^{2}-93d+196)}{3}\,,

(92c)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{5}(d)\varepsilon_{5}^{0}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{2(d-4)^{3}}{3}t+\frac{8(d-4)^{2}}{9}-\frac{16(d-4)(27d^{2}-217d+434)}{9(3d-10)}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(92d)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{5}(d)\varepsilon_{5}^{1}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{8(d^{2}-9d+20)}{3}t^{2}-\frac{4(23d^{2}-195d+410)}{3}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{16(11d^{2}-93d+196)}{3}\,,

(92e)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{5}(d)\varepsilon_{5}^{2}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(d-6)(d-4)^{2}}{144(d-5)}t^{4}+\frac{10d^{3}-133d^{2}+586d-856}{36(d-5)}t^{3}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{-9d^{5}+327d^{4}-3821d^{3}+20111d^{2}-49728d+47180}{18(d-5)(2d-7)(3d-10)}t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2(177d^{4}-2798d^{3}+16759d^{2}-45116d+46060)}{9(6d^{2}-41d+70)}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{8(111d^{4}-1339d^{3}+5186d^{2}-5760d-2688)}{9(6d^{2}-41d+70)}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{128(54d^{4}-810d^{3}+4537d^{2}-11245d+10402)}{9(6d^{2}-41d+70)}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(92f)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{5}(d)\varepsilon_{5}^{3}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{36(d-5)}t^{4}+\frac{-4d^{2}+33d-68}{9(d-5)}t^{3}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2(9d^{4}-201d^{3}+1499d^{2}-4669d+5250)}{9(d-5)(2d-7)(3d-10)}t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{8(33d^{3}-422d^{2}+1809d-2590)}{9(6d^{2}-41d+70)}t-\frac{128(3d^{3}-10d^{2}-53d+182)}{9(6d^{2}-41d+70)}\,,

(92g)

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{\prime}_{5}(d)}{3d-14}\varepsilon_{5}^{4}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{12}t^{3}-\frac{(2d^{2}-17d+36)}{3}t^{2}+\frac{2(2d^{2}-17d+35)}{3}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{8(4d^{2}-31d+59)}{3}\,,

(92h)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{5}(d)\varepsilon_{5}^{5}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(d-6)(d-4)^{2}}{3}t^{3}+2(d^{3}-15d^{2}+74d-120)t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{8(9d^{3}-121d^{2}+536d-780)}{3}t+\frac{32(4d^{3}-52d^{2}+223d-315)}{3}\,,

(92i)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{5}(d)\varepsilon_{5}^{6}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2(d-4)^{2}}{3}t^{4}+\frac{8(2d^{2}-17d+36)}{3}t^{3}-\frac{16(4d^{2}-35d+75)}{3}t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{64(2d^{2}-17d+35)}{3}t\,,

(92j)

with
\kappa_{5}(d;t)\coloneq(d-4)(3d-14)(3d-10)(t-4)^{2}
and
\kappa^{\prime}_{5}(d)\coloneq(d-4)^{2}(d-3)^{2}(3d-14)(3d-10)
.
For
E_{6}
, they are

\displaystyle\kappa_{6}(d;t)\tau_{6}^{30}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-4)^{2}(3d-10)}{192(d-3)}t^{3}+\frac{-3d^{3}+37d^{2}-150d+200}{96(d-3)}t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{-21d^{3}+274d^{2}-1185d+1690}{24(d-3)}t+\frac{24d^{3}-311d^{2}+1329d-1870}{6(d-3)}\,,

(93a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{6}(d;t)\tau_{6}^{21}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(d-4)^{2}(3d-10)}{96}t^{3}+\frac{(3d^{3}-37d^{2}+150d-200)}{48}t^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{12}(45d^{3}-594d^{2}+2585d-3690)t-18d^{3}+233d^{2}-993d+1390\,,

(93b)

\displaystyle\kappa_{6}(d;t)\tau_{6}^{12}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{4(d-5)^{2}(d-3)(3d-10)}{3}t+\frac{4(d-5)(d-3)(3d-10)(5d-23)}{3}\,,

(93c)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{6}(d)\varepsilon_{6}^{0}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{3}+\frac{2(d-4)(9d^{2}-87d+206)}{9(3d-10)}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(93d)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{6}(d)\varepsilon_{6}^{1}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{6d^{3}-79d^{2}+346d-504}{288(d-5)}t^{2}+\frac{6d^{3}-73d^{2}+291d-378}{144(d-5)}t

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{63d^{3}-714d^{2}+2670d-3292}{36(3d-10)}+\frac{4(36d^{3}-465d^{2}+1997d-2850)}{9(3d-10)}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(93e)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{6}(d)\varepsilon_{6}^{2}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{2}-10d+24}{288(d-5)}t^{3}+\frac{-19d^{2}+175d-398}{144(d-5)}t^{2}+\frac{-11d^{2}+93d-186}{36(d-5)}t

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{2(3d^{2}+14d-88)}{9(3d-10)}-\frac{16(27d^{2}-225d+466)}{9(3d-10)}\frac{1}{t}\,,

(93f)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{6}(d)\varepsilon_{6}^{3}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{4-d}{72(d-5)}t^{3}+\frac{7d-29}{36(d-5)}t^{2}+\frac{5d-17}{9(d-5)}t-\frac{16}{9}\,,

(93g)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{6}(d)\varepsilon_{6}^{4}(d;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(d-4)(3d-14)}{24}t^{2}+\frac{(3d-14)(3d-13)}{12}t-\frac{(3d-14)(d^{2}-7d+14)}{3(3d-10)}\,,

(93h)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{6}(d)\varepsilon_{6}^{5}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(d^{2}-10d+24)}{6}t^{2}-\frac{2(d^{2}-11d+30)}{3}t+\frac{4(2d^{2}-19d+45)}{3}\,,

(93i)

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{6}(d)\varepsilon_{6}^{6}(d;t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d-4}{3}t^{3}-\frac{2(3d-13)}{3}t^{2}+\frac{8(d-5)}{3}t\,,

(93j)

with
\kappa_{6}(d;t)\coloneq(d-5)(d-4)(3d-14)(3d-10)(t-4)^{2}
and
\kappa^{\prime}_{6}(d)\coloneq(d-4)(d-3)^{2}(3d-14)
.

Note that, unlike
\varepsilon_{0}^{j}
through
\varepsilon_{4}^{j}
, the coefficients
\varepsilon_{5}^{j}(d;t)
and
\varepsilon_{6}^{j}(d;t)
diverge at
d=4
.
This is why
E_{i}
appear in the divergent parts of
E_{5}(4-2\epsilon;t)
and
E_{6}(4-2\epsilon;t)
, the implications of which are discussed in
section

A.3
.
Note also that
\tau_{5}^{ab}
and
\tau_{6}^{ab}
diverge at the 2-pion threshold,
t=4
.
This potentially problematic behavior is also remedied by the Schouten relations.

Expressions for the master integrals in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions

d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions which are needed for the evaluation of the amplitude.

, as is expected from the cut of the amplitude.

D.2
The tadpole-bubble products in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions

In this section we state the master integrals defined in
eqs.

23
and
24
, which following
eq.

22
can be expressed in terms of the one-loop tadpole [
eq.

19
] and bubble [
eqs.

20
and
21
] integrals.
We state their
\epsilon
expansion to the order needed in this work, which is driven by the presence of
1/\epsilon
counterterms in the two-loop diagrams, and the presence of
T_{30}
in the divergences of the
E_{i}
.
The two-loop masters are

\displaystyle T_{20}(4-2\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{2}{\epsilon}+\Big[3+\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{6}\Big]+\Big[4+\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{3}-\tfrac{2}{3}\zeta(3)\Big]\epsilon+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\,,

(102d)

D.3
The master integral
E_{0}
in
d=4-2\epsilon

The expansion of
E_{0}(4-2\epsilon)
is easily worked out from the parametric representation of the integral and using
HyperInt

Panzer:2014caa
,
or by evaluating the expression in ref.
(
Cacciatori:2023tzp
, eq. (5.48))
using that
\omega_{d}=2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}/\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})
,
or by setting
t=0
in the expressions for
E_{1}(4-2\epsilon;t)
below,
or directly from the dimensional shift in
eq.

83
, requiring only knowledge of
T_{30}(4-2\epsilon)
.
In any case,

E_{0}(4-2\epsilon)=\frac{2}{\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{23}{3\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{\pi^{2}+35}{2\epsilon}+\frac{275+23\pi^{2}-24\zeta(3)}{12}\\
+\left(\frac{89\zeta(3)}{3}+\frac{19\pi^{4}}{240}+\frac{35\pi^{2}}{8}-\frac{189}{8}\right)\epsilon+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\,.

(103)

D.4
The master integrals
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
in
d=4-2\epsilon

We expand these master integrals by directly applying the dimension shifting relations in
eqs.

84
,
85
, and
86
, utilizing the fact that
E_{i}(2;t)
are all finite.
For
E_{1}
, we get

E_{1}(4-2\epsilon;t)=\frac{2}{\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{23-t}{3\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{t^{2}-54t+18\pi^{2}+630}{36\epsilon}+\bar{E}_{1}(4;t)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

(104)

where the finite piece at order
\epsilon^{0}
is

\bar{E}_{1}(4;t)=\frac{t^{3}+24t^{2}-600t+896}{288}E_{1}(2;t)-\frac{t^{4}+12t^{3}-792t^{2}+3968t+1536}{288}E_{2}(2;t)\\
-\frac{(t-16)(t-4)(t^{2}+40t+64)}{144}E_{3}(2;t)+\frac{71t^{2}+18t+6102}{216}+\frac{(23-t)\pi^{2}}{12}-2\zeta(3)\,.

(105)

Likewise for
E_{2}
,

E_{2}(4-2\epsilon;t)=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{28-t}{12\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{24-t+2\pi^{2}}{8\epsilon}+\bar{E}_{2}(4;t)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

(106)

with the finite piece

\bar{E}_{2}(4;t)=\frac{5t^{2}-80t+96}{96}E_{1}(2;t)-\frac{5t^{3}-116t^{2}+376t+896}{96}E_{2}(2;t)\\
-\frac{(t-16)(t-4)(5t+16)}{48}E_{3}(2;t)+\frac{(95t+112)+(28-t)\pi^{2}}{48}-\zeta(3)\,,

(107)

and lastly for
E_{3}
,

E_{3}(4-2\epsilon;t)=-\frac{5}{6\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{t-20}{8\epsilon}+\bar{E}_{3}(4;t)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

(108)

with the finite piece

\bar{E}_{3}(4;t)=\frac{t^{2}-12t-8}{96}E_{1}(2;t)-\frac{t^{3}-24t^{2}+88t+160}{96}E_{2}(2;t)\\
-\frac{(t-16)(t-4)(t+4)}{48}E_{3}(2;t)+\frac{39t-232-10\pi^{2}}{48}\,.

(109)

The expressions for the two-dimensional elliptic master integrals are given in
appendix

E
.

D.6
The master integral
E_{5}
in
d=4-2\epsilon

The expansion of the elliptic master integral
E_{5}
is more subtle, because a direct
application of the dimension shifting relation derived in
section

C.4
gives
1/\epsilon^{2}
and
1/\epsilon
poles depending on the elliptic master integrals in
d=2
.
But the presence of the elliptic master integrals would spoil the renormalizability of the amplitude.
These unwanted contributions are removed using the Schouten relations derived in
section

A.3
.
After using these identities, the
\epsilon
expansion reads

E_{5}(4-2\epsilon;t)=\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{1-3J_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to4.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-2.45348pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}

Unfortunately,

is not simply a combination of

[as is the case for
\bar{E}_{1}(4;t)
, etc.] but also of various
\bar{E}_{n}^{(r)}(2;t)
.
This can possibly be simplified using additional Schouten relations, the derivation of which would itself be a daunting task, and
E_{5}(2;t)
is also not easy to obtain.
Instead, we use
\bar{E}_{5}(4;t)
as-is and evaluate it by solving its differential equation in terms of
E_{1}(2;t)
.

\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.9 0 L 6.26 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{(1)}(\beta)\,,

(116)

and
E_{1}
-dependent (i.e., elliptic) piece

\begin{gathered}\mathscr{S}_{E_{1}}\coloneq\bigg[s_{2}(\beta)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\beta^{2}}+s_{1}(\beta)\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}+s_{0}(\beta)\bigg]E_{1}(2;\beta)\,,\\
s_{0}(\beta)=\frac{2}{3}\,\frac{\beta^{2}(2\beta^{2}-1)}{(\beta^{2}-1)^{2}}\,,\quad s_{1}(\beta)=\frac{2\beta}{3}\frac{3\beta^{4}-7\beta^{2}+3}{\beta^{2}-1}\,,\quad s_{2}(\beta)=\frac{\beta^{2}(4\beta^{2}-3)}{2}\,.\end{gathered}

(117)

We solve this differential equation using the Wrońskian method.
A basis of solutions to the homogeneous equation
[i.e., that obtained by setting
\mathscr{S}_{5}(\beta)=0
in
eq.

113
] is

g_{1}(\beta)\coloneq\beta^{2}-1\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{2}(\beta)\coloneq\frac{\beta^{2}-1}{4}\log\bigg(\frac{\beta+1}{\beta-1}\bigg)-\frac{\beta}{2}\,,

(118)

and the generic solution is then

\bar{E}_{5}(4;\beta)=c_{1}\,g_{1}(\beta)+c_{2}\,g_{2}(\beta)-g_{1}(\beta)\int_{\xi_{1}}^{\beta}\mathscr{S}_{5}(\xi)g_{2}(\xi)\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi^{2}}+g_{2}(\beta)\int_{\xi_{2}}^{\beta}\mathscr{S}_{5}(\xi)g_{1}(\xi)\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi^{2}}\,.

(119)

The limits
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are arbitrary as long as the constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are adjusted accordingly, which can be done by ensuring that the
\beta=\infty
(i.e.,
t=0
) limit from
section

B.5
is reproduced.
Such adjustment is very subtle in practice, and one particular implementation will be discussed in forthcoming work
LLSV
.

The elliptic piece would be simpler if it only involved
E_{1}
, not its derivatives, so we apply integration by parts.
Defining

h_{n}(\xi)\coloneq\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\xi^{2}}\frac{s_{2}(\xi)g_{n}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\frac{s_{1}(\xi)g_{n}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}+\frac{s_{0}(\xi)g_{n}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}\,,

(120)

or, explicitly,

\displaystyle h_{1}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{54\xi^{6}-57\xi^{4}+11\xi^{2}-6}{3\xi^{2}(\xi^{2}-1)}\,,

(121)

\displaystyle h_{2}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\xi^{2}-1)\big(54\xi^{6}-57\xi^{4}+11\xi^{2}-6\big)\log\Big(\frac{\xi+1}{\xi-1}\Big)-2\xi\big(54\xi^{6}-93\xi^{4}+31\xi^{2}+6\big)}{12(\xi^{2}-1)^{2}\xi^{2}}\,,

we have

g_{2}(\beta)\int_{\xi_{2}}^{\beta}\mathscr{S}_{E_{1}}(\xi)g_{1}(\xi)\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi^{2}}-g_{1}(\beta)\int_{\xi_{1}}^{\beta}\mathscr{S}_{E_{1}}(\xi)g_{2}(\xi)\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi^{2}}\\
=g_{2}(\beta)\int_{\xi_{2}}^{\beta}h_{1}(\xi)E_{1}(2;\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi-g_{1}(\beta)\int_{\xi_{1}}^{\beta}h_{2}(\xi)E_{1}(2;\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi+\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}E_{1}(2;\beta)+\Delta_{E_{1}}\,,

(122)

where
\Delta_{E_{1}}
incorporates all boundary terms from the lower integration limit; we will omit it from now on since it can be absorbed into
c_{1}g_{1}(\beta)+c_{2}g_{2}(\beta)
.
At the upper integration limit, all boundary terms cancel except for
\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}\big[g_{1}(\beta)g^{\prime}_{2}(\beta)-g^{\prime}_{1}(\beta)g_{2}(\beta)\big]E_{1}(2;\beta)
, where the factor in brackets is equal to
1
.

For brevity in the following, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}[\beta;G_{1},G_{2}]\coloneq

\displaystyle-G_{1}\int_{\xi_{1}}^{\beta}\bigg[\frac{\mathscr{S}_{J}(\xi)+\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{rat}}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}g_{2}(\xi)+E_{1}(2;\xi)h_{2}(\xi)\bigg]\mathrm{d}\xi

(123)

\displaystyle+G_{2}\int_{\xi_{2}}^{\beta}\bigg[\frac{\mathscr{S}_{J}(\xi)+\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{rat}}(\xi)}{\xi^{2}}g_{1}(\xi)+E_{1}(2;\xi)h_{1}(\xi)\bigg]\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

The reason for introducing
G_{1},G_{2}
will become apparent in
section

D.7
.
Thus,

\bar{E}_{5}(4;\beta)=c_{1}g_{1}(\beta)+c_{2}g_{2}(\beta)+\frac{s_{2}(\beta)}{\beta^{2}}E_{1}(2;\beta)+\mathcal{I}\big[\beta;g_{1}(\beta),g_{2}(\beta)\big]\,.

(124)

While this form of
E_{5}(4;\beta)
involving a one-dimensional integral is much more tractable than the original
12
-dimensional Feynman integral,
the evaluation of
\mathcal{I}[\beta;G_{1},G_{2}]
is a formidable task in and of itself, not least because it depends on the ability to evaluate
E_{1}(2;\xi)
across the entire integration range.
This is detailed in forthcoming work
LLSV
.

D.7
The master integral
E_{6}
at
d=4-2\epsilon

As for
E_{5}
, the Schouten relations must be applied to the
\epsilon
expansion of this integral.
After doing that, it reads

Appendix E
Expressions for the master integrals in two dimensions

At this point, all master integrals have been rewritten in terms of
J_{\hbox to9.45pt{\vbox to4.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.72401pt\lower-2.45348pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}
{{}}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.9 0 C 3.9 1.72 2.15 3.12 0 3.12 C -2.15 3.12 -3.9 1.72 -3.9 0 C -3.9 -1.72 -2.15 -3.12 0 -3.12 C 2.15 -3.12 3.9 -1.72 3.9 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.3}{0.0}{0.0}{0.3}{2.81685pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.9 0 L -6.26 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.9 0 L 6.26 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{(n)}(t)
(given in
section

D.2
) and
E_{1}(2;t)
, so it remains to describe this “master of master integrals”.
It has been extensively studied and is known in several different forms, among which is the Bessel integral representation
Groote:2005ay
;
Bailey:2008ib
;
Bloch:2014qca
, which holds for
t<16
:

E_{1}(2;t)=-8\int_{0}^{\infty}xI_{0}(x\sqrt{t})\big[K_{0}(x)\big]^{4}\mathrm{d}x\,,

(129)

where
I_{\alpha}(z)
and
K_{\alpha}(z)
are the modified Bessel functions of the first and second kind, respectively.
This has as a special case
E_{0}(2)=E_{1}(2;0)=7\zeta(3)
.

Combining
eq.

129
with
eqs.

69
and
70
gives

\displaystyle E_{2}(2;t)

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\infty}x\Big\{I_{0}(x\sqrt{t})+\tfrac{x\sqrt{t}}{2}I_{1}(x\sqrt{t})\Big\}\big[K_{0}(x)\big]^{4}\mathrm{d}x\,,

(130)

\displaystyle E_{3}(2;t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}x\Big\{(2+x^{2})I_{0}(x\sqrt{t})+\tfrac{x(t+2)}{\sqrt{t}}I_{1}(x\sqrt{t})+x^{2}I_{2}(x\sqrt{t})\Big\}\big[K_{0}(x)\big]^{4}\mathrm{d}x\,.

(131)

When
t<0
, the integrals are kept real by
\sqrt{t}=i\sqrt{|t|}
and
I_{\alpha}(iz)=i^{\alpha}J_{\alpha}(z)
, where
J_{\alpha}
is the (unmodified) Bessel function of the first kind.

The Bessel integral formulation is straightforward, but it is limited to
t<16
and is numerically rather slow.
The formulation in terms of elliptic functions that is the main result of ref.
Bloch:2014qca
is applicable everywhere in the complex plane, but is much more complicated to describe, requiring much higher mathematics than what is used elsewhere in this paper.
They are also given in terms of variables whose relationship to our
t
is highly nontrivial, and, while known in the literature
Bloch:2014qca
, require adjustments before being practical.
To keep the scope of the present paper manageable, we therefore defer its description to forthcoming work
LLSV
.

\displaystyle\quad+8L_{\pi}\big(2l_{5}^{q}-l_{6}^{q}\big)-8tc_{56}^{q}-32c_{34}^{q}\,.

(133)

For the N
3
LO expression, we use the same thematic subdivision as in
eq.

30
:

\Pi_{T}^{\mathrm{N^{3}LO}}(t)=\Pi_{E}(t)+\Pi_{J}(t)+\Pi_{l}(t)+\Pi_{c}(t)+\Pi_{\zeta}(t)+r_{0}^{q}+tr_{1}^{q}+t^{2}r_{2}^{q}\,.

(134)

Due to how the contributions are subdivided,
\Pi_{E}(t)=\bar{\Pi}_{E}(t)
and
\Pi_{J}(t)=\bar{\Pi}_{J}(t)
[see
eqs.

31
and
32
].
For the remaining parts, we have

\displaystyle\Pi_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=\bar{\Pi}_{l}(t)-4L_{\pi}^{2}\big(2l_{1}^{q}-l_{2}^{q}+12l_{5}^{q}-6l_{6}^{q}\big)+\frac{2-4L_{\pi}}{3}\big(l_{1}^{q}+2l_{2}^{q}\big)+16L_{\pi}l_{4}^{q}\big(2l_{5}^{q}-l_{6}^{q}\big)\,,

(135)

\displaystyle\Pi_{c}(t)

\displaystyle=\bar{\Pi}_{c}(t)-\Big(192c_{6}^{q}+48c_{29}^{q}-16c_{30}^{q}+12c_{31}^{q}+12c_{32}^{q}-8c_{33}^{q}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad-192c_{34}^{q}+32c_{35}^{q}-32c_{44}^{q}+64c_{46}^{q}-128c_{47}^{q}+32c_{50}^{q}\Big)L_{\pi}

\displaystyle+6c_{31}^{q}+6c_{32}^{q}-4c_{33}^{q}-16c_{44}^{q}+64(l_{3}^{q}-l_{4}^{q})c_{34}^{q}\,,

(136)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\zeta}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3t^{2}-13t-6}{27t}\zeta(3)-\frac{t^{2}+18t}{81}L_{\pi}^{3}-\frac{20t-207}{27}L_{\pi}^{2}-\bigg[\frac{17t^{2}}{432}-\frac{845t}{648}+\frac{703}{54}\bigg]L_{\pi}

\displaystyle-\bigg[\frac{t^{2}}{216}-\frac{5t}{27}+\frac{22}{27}+\frac{1}{18t}\bigg]\pi^{2}+\frac{20245t^{2}}{139968}-\frac{55511t}{23328}-\frac{65195}{3888}+\frac{1216}{27t}\,.

(137)

In order to obtain the physical result presented in
section

4
, the
t\to 0
limit of the above expressions must be taken and subtracted.

\displaystyle\Pi_{T}^{\mathrm{NLO}}(0)=-\frac{5+3L_{\pi}}{9}-4l_{5}^{q}-8h_{2}^{q}\,,\qquad\Pi_{T}^{\mathrm{NNLO}}(0)=8L_{\pi}\big(2l_{5}^{q}-l_{6}^{q}\big)-32c_{34}^{q}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{J}(0)=\frac{4-\pi^{2}}{36}\,.

(139)

For N
3
LO, we also need the expansion
eq.

79
of the elliptic master integrals.
This gives

\Pi_{E}(t)=-\bigg[\frac{1216}{27}+\frac{2\zeta(3)}{9}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{18}\bigg]\frac{1}{t}+\bigg[\frac{52069}{1944}-\frac{7957\zeta(3)}{432}+\frac{91\pi^{2}}{108}\bigg]+\mathcal{O}(t)\,,

(140)

whose pole cancels against that of
\Pi_{\zeta}(t)
.
Subtracting
\Pi_{J}(0)
and the finite part of
\Pi_{E}(t)
from
\Pi_{\zeta}(0)
produces
\bar{\Pi}_{\zeta}(t)
.

Appendix G
Implementation details

/

expansion

Dimension shift

Result!

M_{\pi}
/
F_{\pi}
renormalization

lower-order amplitudes

HVP*LO.frm
ChPTlib
HVP.frm
Topologies
Feynman rules
T_{ij}
E_{i}
E_{6}
LECs
NLO, NNLO

Figure 2
:

A sketch of the steps involved in the HVP calculations, and the programs performing these steps.
For NLO and NNLO, only a subset of the steps are of course performed.
With the exception of third-party programs like
FORM

Vermaseren:2000nd
;
Ruijl:2017dtg
and
LiteRed 2

Lee:2012cn
;
Lee:2013mka
, all necessary code can be found in the linked repositories
chptlib
;
hvpfiles
.

The calculation of the amplitude was carried out using the
FORM
library
ChPTlib

chptlib
, which during the preparation of this work evolved from Mattias Sjö’s personal
FORM
files (themselves inspired by Johan Bijnens’ ditto) into something more generally usable (albeit still limited and under development).
We therefore choose to make it available, along with the implementation of the present HVP calculation
hvpfiles
, so that the reader may reproduce our results.
In this appendix, we briefly describe how to do that.
A graphical outline is given in
fig.

2
, and a step-by-step summary in the provided
README
file.
26
26

26

Questions regarding the implementation should primarily be directed to Mattias Sjö.

ChPTlib
is mainly a collection of
FORM
procedures, containing an implementation of the bare and renormalized ChPT Lagrangian
Gasser:1983yg
;
Gasser:1984gg
;
Bijnens:1999hw
;
Bijnens:1999sh
;
Bijnens:2018lez
and utilities for processing ChPT amplitudes.
It also contains a Python program
ChPT.py
for automatically generating the necessary
FORM
code given a description of the diagram topologies (it is currently not interoperable with automatic Feynman diagram generators).
The diagram description format is intended to be quickly transcribed by hand from a sketch of the diagrams, and to express the momentum routing in a way that directly translates into the
I_{\vec{\nu}}
notation of
section

3
.
The diagrams in
tables

1
and
2
are found in this format in
HVP*LO.chpt
(with
*
standing for
N
,
NN
or
N3
), and running
ChPT.py --form HVP*LO.chpt
generates the
FORM
code for deriving the Feynman rules, performing all Wick contractions, and symmetrizing.
This is then applied by running the
FORM
program
HVP*LO.frm
, which is generated with
ChPT.py --form-main HVP*LO.chpt
and then supplanted with, among other things, an implementation of
eq.

3
.

With the diagrams generated, all subsequent steps in the calculation are performed by running the
FORM
program
HVP.frm
.
The most time-consuming step is the master integral reduction, which is done by converting the raw amplitude into a Mathematica program that invokes
LiteRed 2

Lee:2012cn
;
Lee:2013mka
, running it, and converting the result back to
FORM
.
After that, the other manipulations described in
section

3
are performed as outlined in
fig.

2
.
Of particular note is the standalone file
tadbub.frm
, which broadcasts the
\epsilon
-expansion (see
section

D.2
) of the tadpole and bubble integrals, thereby minimizing the risk of clashes between sign and normalization conventions in the various places where this expansion is needed.
The output of
HVP.frm
is only some
L
a
T
e
X
formatting away from
eqs.

28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
34
,
35
, and
33
, and most of the crosschecks listed in
section

4
can be automatically performed by setting various flags listed at the top of the file.
